# ScoreReader worklog

This file is the record of the work, round by round: what was measured on each set of test pages, what the reader
cannot do yet, and what comes next. A round is one pull request of rules, and it has the number of its pull
request. [README.md](../README.md) says what ScoreReader is and how it works; the figures of each round are written
down here.

## Results on every score of LitChoir (the wide test)

The wide test reads every score of the app that has a typed hymn to compare with: 369 scores on 657 pages, 516 of
them scans. 364 are counted: the page of 5 scans holds another version than the typed hymn. Notes right means pitch
and length both right. The count is of the notes that the pages show: 5,865 typed notes that no page prints are
listed apart. Most are recited syllables and the held notes of an ison that the page does not mark; 343 are a
refrain or a repeat that the page prints once and the typed hymn writes out again. 218 typed notes that a part
sings from another part's staff are not compared. Each score is read as the `hymn` command reads it: its pages in
order, with one vote for its key. Where a typed hymn gives a note otherwise than its page prints, the note counts as
the page prints it (since 15 November 2026, see below; 129 notes so far, 93 of them on the clean copies).

| Kind of score | Scores | Notes right | At 99 % or more | At 90 % or more | Under 50 % |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PDFs that notation software wrote (read from their drawing) | 70 | 99.3 % (20,016 of 20,163) | 53 | 70 | 0 |
| Scans | 294 | 84.2 % (58,691 of 69,700) | 42 | 118 | 17 |
| All | 364 | 87.6 % (78,707 of 89,863) | 95 | 188 | 17 |

The syllables of the own letter reader were last counted before round 47. The mistakes that are left are many small causes;
  the largest that were found
  since: two voices a second apart that the print sets as one blot of ink brought 41 notes on the clean copies

Of 30 mistakes drawn by lot and looked at
  on the page in round 12,
  8 are a hollow head and a filled head mixed up, 4 a dot, 4 a tie or a slur, 4 a beam, 4 a sign, and 6 one of a
  kind (a note that a pencil crossed out, a typed hymn that differs from its page). Two fine engravings held about
  240 of the 1,269 notes that were lost then (1,017 now, with the notes of chords counted). An earlier count said that a quarter of the mistakes are
  the scorer lining a staff up with the wrong part: the tool behind that count lined the notes up in its own way,
  and it is not held any more.

  92.6 % on the software-made PDFs (their words are text in the PDF; Tesseract is not used there), and 88.3 % on
  the scans (19,957 of 22,591; round 84 brought 11 and lost none, by a letter that a reading left out between two words, a digit that was read behind a word, and an O that was read as a sign, and its count holds 1 more, an O at the edge of a refrain that one page prints once; round 83 brought 38, 42 gained and 4 lost by seven rules for the words: a stop between two letters, a piece with no vowel, two letters that touch, a slur that touches a word and an open word that the list parts so; round 82 brought 4, by a word that goes on over the end of a page and a 1 that is an i; round 76 brought 13, 6 by two syllables of one read piece that stand under one note and 7 by a row that goes on from the word that the row before leaves open, round 75 brought 1 with the notes, on a clean copy, and counts 10 more in the refrain that one page prints once more, round 74 changed no syllable, round 73 brought 1 with the notes, on a copy by hand, round 72 changed no syllable, round 71 changed no syllable, round 70 brought 5, by a word that goes on from the row before it, round 68 lost 1 with the notes, on a clean copy whose last chord the count splits crosswise now, round 67 brought 1, by a word that a hyphen leaves open at the end of a row, rounds 63 to 66 changed no syllable, round 62 counts 5 more in the stretch that round 37 had left out, rounds 54 and 55 changed no syllable, round 53 took 3: a rule for a stray word under the clef gains 1 syllable on the clean copies and loses 3 on one copy by hand, and 1 goes with a note; round 52 brought 3, by the word O beside a bracket and with the notes, round 51 brought 27, by a word that every reading of its row holds alike and with the notes, round 50 brought 10 by two rules for the words and 1 by the list of the words by the page, round 48 brought 1 with the notes, round 47 brought 167 with the notes, by the staves and the systems of copies by hand that it reads now, round 44 lost 6 of them with the notes of one copy by hand, round 40 brought 68, round 39 brought 196, the rules of round 37 brought 25 and its count by the page 26, the
  rules of round 36 brought 471, the rules of round 35 brought 138 and

  words are compared in the layout that the count of the notes chooses for a score, and the scorer sets a few
  scores in another layout or on another part of their pages now. On the clean copies 97.3 % of the syllables and 95.3 % of the whole words are
  right, and 45 of the 78 scores have 99 % of their syllables (44 before round 84, 40 before round 83, 39 before round 82, 37 before round 76, 36 before round 70, 31 before round 47, 28 before round 39, 25 before round 36, 20 before round 35, 15 in round 10, 4 in round 9); 155 syllables
  are lacking for 99 % of each clean score (158 after round 83, 168 after round 82, 170 after round 76, 175 after round 75, 191 after round 47, 195 before it, 200 before round 40, 227 before round 39, 285 before round 37, 366 before round 36). The
  list of words is made from the typed hymns of the app without the hymn that is read and its other scores, so no

  With the own letter reader, which uses no model, the scans have about 40 %; its column in the table was measured
  in round 7 and not again since.
- The count of the syllables changed in round 10, and that alone moved the figures: the syllables of a refrain
  that the page prints once and the typed hymn writes out again are listed apart now, as the notes of such a
  refrain already were (150 syllables on 2 scores). Counted the new way, the clean copies stood at 85.0 % before
  that round (5,302 of 6,240; 83.3 % by the old count) and all scores at 78.2 % (22,449 of 28,697).
- The words are counted in three ways. Syllables right: the typed syllables that the reader wrote, in the typed
  order. Whole words right: the same for words, each joined from its syllables at the hyphens that the reader
  read; a word counts only when all of it is right, and a word that the page prints with no hyphen over two notes
  is still one word. Letters in order (90 % on the scans with Tesseract, 96 % on the clean copies): a lenient
  count, since two unlike hymn texts of one length share two fifths of their letters in order. Under which note a syllable stands is not
  scored yet.
- A note can hold several syllables (a reciting note, or a syllable whose note was not read), and the words of
  such a note are counted one by one, in the reading and in the typed hymn. The figures of the own letter reader
  on the thick copies and the copies by hand are from before this count.
- Some typed syllables stand on no page: a refrain that the page prints once and the typed hymn writes out after
  each verse. The two clean scores that miss the most syllables are of this kind. Such syllables are not counted
  apart yet, as the notes of a repeat are.
- The sorting into clean, thick and by hand was done by eye from the first system of each score. Some typed hymns
  differ from their page in ways that the scorer was not told, and count as wrong: a tie that the page prints and
  the typed hymn does not, a refrain, a note that a pencil changed.
- The scorer gives each staff of a page the typed parts that it lines up with best. A small change in the reading
  can turn that choice: one hymn lost 16 notes in an earlier round because one wrong note was no longer
  read, and the scorer then took its staff for one part and not for two.
- The figures count parts, not staves. A staff that holds two parts is split: the top note of each chord goes to
  the upper part and the bottom note to the lower. Until round 17 that was all: of a typed part that
  divides only the top or the bottom note was counted, and a false note in a chord took the place of the true
  one. Now the other notes of a chord are counted after that line-up (see the round of the count below). The line-up itself
  has no sense of time: it compares the notes of a part in their order.
- Round 84 (21 November 2026): two half notes at a thick ledger dash, the wire of a paperclip, a letter that a
  reading of a row left out, and a digit behind a word.

  The round took the clean scores that are one place away from whole. The 47 clean scores that were not whole were
  ranked by what each lacks: pascha-receive-short lacked two notes at one place, liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 read one
  note that is not there and lacked one syllable, nativity-canon-ode-9 lacks one syllable and nativity-canon-ode-1
  two (the open question of the count). Two rules for the notes and three for the words came of it, and
  pascha-receive-short and liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 are whole now. Both rules for the notes are rules for one
  case of LitChoir's pages; each was counted with its own code on every scan page and on the fresh set.

  - **A half note on a thick ledger dash and a half note on the staff's first line, as one piece of ink**
    (`symbols.DASH_HOLLOW`). pascha-receive-short prints a bass chord of two half notes, the upper one on the
    ledger line over the staff and the lower one on the top line, a third apart, and the print's thick ledger dash
    glues them into one piece. A piece that is higher than a head is cut at the valley of its rows, and the dash is
    the heaviest row of the piece: the cut ran through the inside of the lower head, and neither note was
    read. Never cutting through closed paper is no rule: 909 cuts of the scan pages run through
    closed paper (186 on the clean copies), and for most pieces it is the only cut. The rule asks for the whole
    shape: one level bar 1.8 heads wide or more and at most 0.45 staff spaces thick, 0.85 to 1.3 staff spaces
    beside the staff, a piece 1.9 to 2.4 heads high that ends half a head past the dash and half a head past the
    staff's line, closed paper in both halves, and a cut of today through that paper. Such a piece is cut anew in
    the middle between the dash and the line, the upper part is kept to the columns of its head, and the two
    heads keep their places and the dash. A census of every piece with a dash beside the staff: 444 pieces as high
    as two heads, 111 with one thick level bar, and 7 with the two heads' shape. One of the 7 is cut through a
    head; the six others are pairs of whole notes, pairs that are cut rightly at their valley, and a sharp before
    a pair. A first
    census with a bar of 1.6 heads missed that piece itself: the row with the stem in it is 1.71 heads wide.
    pascha-receive-short reads 153 of 153 notes (151 before) and no note that is not there. With its own code the
    rule acts at this one place on the 485 scan pages and nowhere on the 258 pages of the fresh set.
  - **The wire of a paperclip** (`layers.CLIP`, `symbols.CLIP_CELL`). liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 read a half note
    that is not there: the bow of a paperclip hangs into the first staff and closes a cell with a staff line and
    the stem of the note below. The wire is grey where the print is black. A census of every thick grey piece of
    the scan pages (the ink 2 px inside its edge, between 0.12 and 0.7 of the way from the page's ink to its
    paper, a staff space squared or more and four staff spaces long): 151 pieces on 40 pages, each kind looked at.
    14 pages hold a paperclip, 7 of them across a staff; the others are the rings of a binder, the shadow at a
    page's edge, the pale staff lines and beams of pale prints, the grey pages of one score, and one slur of
    a pale print. The wire is a band of one width that turns back: 0.45 to 1.0 staff spaces thick, whole in 0.6
    or more when it is opened with a disc of 0.6 of its thickness (the clips 0.70 to 0.98, a binder's rings 0.44),
    8 staff spaces long or more, and 3.5 thicknesses or more across its narrow side (the shadows and the pale
    lines are straight). The slur is such a band too, bent and whole: it is 0.31 staff spaces thick, and the
    limit of the thickness is what leaves it. 7 wires on 7 pages pass; the other clips are too dark, too pale,
    blurred, in pieces or two wires side by side, and stay as they were. The fresh set holds no thick grey ink at
    all (no piece on 258 pages).

    The first form took the wire out of the ink, with its rim. The false note went, and by the count no note was
    gained or lost on the 40 pages. Set beside each other note by note, the pages showed more: on a page of
    liturgy-mercy-of-peace--s2 three clips lie over the clef and the key signature of the first staff, the wire
    had cut the first sharp from the second, the key signature of that staff is not found, and the sharp that now
    stood free was read as two notes; the count lined them up with two typed notes that another clip hides, so no
    figure moved. On two more pages the start of a staff and the ends of three stems moved by a few pixels (the
    page's measure of broken strokes changes when two long strokes go). The wire stays in the ink now. Its mask is
    a layer, and one later step asks it: a hollow mark is no head where the wire is 0.3 or more of the ink around
    its closed paper (0.69 at the one place; the inside of a head is closed by the head's own ink). So read, the
    rule changes one note of one page, liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 reads no note that is not there, and every other
    page reads as on main. A rule for one case, and a mask that the next rules for a clip can ask.
  - **A letter that a reading of a row left out** (`ocr.UNREAD`). The same score lacked the "O" of "unto Thee, O
    Lord": a lone capital between two words. A row is read at three sizes, and a reading that holds only words of
    the list ends the row. The first size read the O as nothing, its reading held only words of the list, and the
    sizes that read the O were not read. The ink tells: a piece between two words of the reading that is 0.3 to 1.5
    heights of the row wide, has ink in 0.85 of the row's rows, goes on at most 0.6 heights over the row and 0.5
    under it (a bar line and a stem go on), stands 0.25 heights from both words and has strokes 0.6 to 1.6 times
    those of the row. Such a reading is not whole, and the other sizes are read. A bar line that was read as a
    stroke has a box that begins inside the letter beside it, so a token of strokes alone covers no letter; a
    form in which no token without a letter covered one fired at the sign of a copyright line and lost a
    syllable. Reading every size although one is whole was counted too: 22 syllables gained and 8 lost on all scan
    pages, with about 45 new stray words of one or two letters: no rule. The rule acts at this one row of
    LitChoir's scan pages (1 syllable gained, none lost) and at one row of the fresh set, where no word changes.
  - **A digit behind the letters of a word** (`ocr.DIGIT_END`). liturgy-mercy-of-peace read "THY4" where a bar
    line runs through the Y, and communion-give-thanks "a4." at the end of an Alleluia. A census of every token
    with letters and a digit from 2 to 9 on the scan pages (298 tokens, 95 with the digit at the end), labelled by
    the typed hymn and looked at tile by tile: on a print the digit is a stroke beside the word, a 4 or a 7 (a
    bar line, a full stop, the end of a slur); on a copy by hand it is a letter, a 5 for an s in every one of 13
    tokens ("a5" is "as"), a 2 for an a or an e. A first form took every digit off where the letters before it
    are a word of the list: 4 words better, and "a5" became "a" in four rows of copies by hand, level in the count
    and wrong on the page. The rule has two halves now: a 4 or a 7 behind a word of the list goes, and a 5 is an
    s where the list holds the word with it. The digits with a bow of their own stay. The first half: 2
    syllables gained on the clean copies and 3 on all scan pages, none lost; the second: none on the clean copies
    and 6 on all scan pages (four copies by hand), none lost. One word that the first half changes is stray ink
    before and after ("a4" under a stem of pascha-canon-ode-8).
  - **An O that is read as the sign of a copyright line with a bracket** (`ocr.ROUND_SIDE`). The ring of a large O
    is read as "©)" as it is read as "@)": of 4 such tokens of the scan pages 3 are the word O, and the one that
    is not stands in a reading of a copy by hand that is not kept. The sign without a bracket stays under its
    older rule. 1 syllable gained on a clean copy (non-liturgical-o-pure-virgin--s2), none lost.
  - Looked at, no rule. A hyphen that Tesseract reads with a stray sign beside it (the "=———=—+" of round 83): of
    390 such places on the scan pages the token is a true hyphen at 34 and wrong at 26, and the others change
    nothing, so the token alone does not tell. nativity-canon-ode-9 still lacks its "in" under a loop in pencil:
    no stored reading holds it. wedding-alleluia-grechaninov reads two notes that are not there in a grey streak
    that runs across a staff; the stored measure of how pale the core of a note is counts the paper in the box of
    a flagged note (of the clean notes from 0.2 on, 129 are right and 6 are not there), so a rule for grey ink
    needs a census of its own.
  - By the quick count the words rules together gain 4 syllables on the clean copies and 11 on all scan pages
    and lose none, and no word of the fresh set changes; each rule was counted off alone against all rules on.
  - The full reading with the words, every scan page with Tesseract (`w377v1` against `w376v1`; the lists by the
    page and the scorer are as in round 83): 2 notes gained and none lost (pascha-receive-short, 151 to 153 of
    153), and 1 note that is not there is gone (liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2); no layout, no window and no count of
    the notes set apart differs in any score. The notes differ on 12 pages: the page of the dash, the page of the
    clip's cell, and the pages of the words. Clean copies 22,090 of 22,649 notes (97.5 %; 41 at 99 %, 40 before;
    21,958 of 22,605 as typed); 6,084 of 6,255 syllables (97.3 %; 6,020 of 6,260 as typed) and 4,315 of 4,528
    whole words (95.3 %): 4 syllables gained and none lost (communion-give-thanks, liturgy-mercy-of-peace,
    liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 and non-liturgical-o-pure-virgin--s2, 1 each), 5 whole words gained and none lost.
    The count grows by one syllable, in communion-give-thanks (204 to 205): the O that is read now stands at the
    edge of a stretch that the count sets apart as a refrain that the page prints once, and it was set apart with
    it while it was not read (109 syllables apart, 108 now). The base scored as typed gives the figures of round
    83 exactly (21,956 of 22,605 and 6,016 of 6,259). 38 clean scores read every printed note (554 lacking; 37 and
    556 before), 44 every syllable (171 lacking; 43 and 174), 33 both (31), 40 have no note that is not there (216
    with the places that a hand changed apart, 221 without; 39, 217 and 222) and 33 are whole in all three (31):
    liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 and pascha-receive-short are new. 4 clean scores lack one to three notes (5), and
    440 notes are lacking for 99 % of each (441). 45 clean scores have 99 % of their syllables (44), and 155
    syllables are lacking for 99 % of each (158). By the count before round 78 the clean copies hold 417 notes
    that are not there (419), 407 with the places that a hand changed apart (409). All scores 78,707 of 89,863
    notes (87.6 %; 95 at 99 %, 94 before) and 2,514 that are not there (2,515); syllables 25,610 of 28,696 (89.2
    %; 25,424 of 28,701 as typed), 11 gained and none lost, and whole words 16,915 of 20,591, 10 gained and none
    lost. The 7 syllables outside the clean copies: troparia-holy-fathers 3, ascension-holy-fathers and its second
    scan, forerunner-beheading-kontakion and pascha-stichera 1 each. The words of the two full passes differ at
    the 11 places of the quick count and nowhere else. Scans 58,691 of 69,700 notes (84.2 %; 42 at 99 %, 41
    before), 19,957 of 22,591 syllables (88.3 %) and 13,986 of 16,242 whole words; thick copies 1,771 of 1,980 and
    1,207 of 1,371, as before; copies by hand 12,102 of 14,356 and 8,464 of 10,343. Staff by staff 76,058 of
    92,680 notes (76,054), on the clean copies 21,177 of 23,090 (21,173): 2 each in the two scores of the notes
    rules. The kit: 6,232 of 6,901 by parts and 5,800 of 6,419 staff by staff, as before. The fresh set, every
    page read with the words (`fr377` against `fr376`): no note, no syllable and no whole word differs (39,957 of
    41,761 notes, 95.7 %, and 630 that are not there; 8,344 of 11,341 syllables, 73.6 %; 5,917 of 6,647 whole
    words, 89.0 %). Its pages hold no thick grey ink and no pair at a thick dash, and the rules for the words
    change no word there. The repository's files are the measured copy, byte for byte.
- Round 83 (21 November 2026): seven rules for the words: a stop between two letters, a piece of a word with no
  vowel, two letters that touch, a slur that touches a word, and an open word that the list parts so.

  The round stayed with the clean scores that read every printed note, read none that is not there, and are not
  whole by their words alone: carol-great-rejoicing (4 syllables lacking), memorial-troparia--s2 (3),
  nativity-canon-ode-1 (2), nativity-canon-ode-9 (1) and liturgy-mercy-of-peace (18). Each lacking syllable was
  traced through every stored reading of its row. Two scores lack words that are a hand's: nativity-canon-ode-1
  the printed words that a hand crossed out (the open question of the count), and liturgy-mercy-of-peace words that
  a hand wrote on a pasted slip. The others are the reader's, and seven rules of the words step came of them. No
  rule touches the notes: only `scorereader/ocr.py` changes. carol-great-rejoicing and memorial-troparia--s2 are
  whole now. Each rule was counted by the quick count from stored rows, with the rule off against all rules on, on
  the clean copies, on all scan pages and on the fresh set, and the full passes were read at the end.

  - **A stop between two letters ends a word** (`ocr.STOP_INSIDE`). memorial-troparia--s2 read "Lord,where" as one
    piece: the print sets the two words close, and Tesseract gave one word. Such a piece was parted only where the
    list holds both halves (`ocr.ENDS`), and the hymn that is read is left out of the list. A comma, a full stop or
    another stop that stands between two letters now ends a word. The census of the stored rows: 51 such tokens on
    LitChoir's scan pages, 18 of them two typed words of their hymn, the others labels and addresses ("S.S.A.",
    "www.PODOBEN.com") and rows of stray ink. Clean copies 2 syllables gained and none lost; all scan pages 7 gained
    and 3 lost, 8 whole words more (the losses are rows of two copies by hand where another reading of the row then
    wins: liturgy-first-antiphon 2, cherubic-voronkoff 1); the fresh set 29 gained and none lost. On the fresh set
    its whole words are 19 gained and 8 lost. The 8 stand at a hyphen that Tesseract read as a full stop or a
    colon inside a word ("sâr.ba", "Domnu.lui", "In:tru"): before, such a word was one piece with its syllables
    wrong and counted as a whole word; now its syllables count and the word is two, and in one row another reading
    wins with it. No token of LitChoir's pages has such a stop at any size (a census of every stop between two
    letters, labelled by the typed words), so no guard was written, as the rules for the fresh set say.
  - **A piece between a cut and a hyphen holds a vowel** (`ocr.CUT_VOWEL`). carol-great-rejoicing read "An-gels" as
    "An-g" and "els". The step that parts two words at a wide gap inside one read word (`ocr.CUT`) counts the
    pieces of ink against the signs that Tesseract read, and where two letters touch (the A and the n) the pieces
    stand a sign off: the gap after the hyphen cut the word a letter early. A piece with no vowel between the cut
    and the next hyphen is no syllable, and such a cut is not made ("UN-DI-VI" and "D-ED" in liturgy-we-have-seen,
    where the print sets the letters of one syllable wide apart). Clean copies 2 gained and none lost; all scan
    pages 4 and 1 (pascha-canon-ode-6, a noisy row where another reading wins); the fresh set 4 and 1. The one of
    the fresh set is a Romanian "ei l-au", whose l before its hyphen is a word: the rule acts only on a page whose
    words fit the list, and that page fits it by its rows of "la la la". It is said here and left.
  - **A piece with no vowel is no word** (`ocr.ENDS_VOWEL`). pascha-hypakoe and its second scan read "sav-eth" as
    "save" and "th": a list that is made from typed hymns holds "th" as a word, from a "wi-th" that a hymn spreads
    under two notes. A piece of two letters or more with no vowel is not cut off as a word. Two first forms went
    wrong, and the fresh set showed both. The test took every letter outside the plain alphabet for a consonant
    (56 syllables and 90 whole words lost there): it judges the plain letters only now, on a page whose words fit
    the list. And it sorted the ways to part a word before the test for one way, so that of two ways one was left
    and a word that had stayed whole was parted ("in-tru"): it looks at the one way that was found and only takes
    its cut away. Clean copies 2 gained and none lost; all scan pages 15 and 1 (st-basil-champion-leader--s2, a
    copy by hand), 10 whole words more; the fresh set 8 and none.
  - **The first word of a row goes on from the open word** (`ocr.ENDS_CARRIED`). memorial-troparia--s2 ends a row
    with "a-" and begins the next with "lone", which was parted into "lo" and "ne", two words of the list. The
    first word of a row is not parted where it is a word of the list together with the word that the row before
    leaves open (`ocr.MEND_BEFORE`). 1 syllable gained on the clean copies and on all scan pages, none lost; no
    word of the fresh set changes.
  - **Two letters that touch, read as one** (`ocr.MEND_TWIN`). carol-great-rejoicing read "born" as "bom": an r and
    an n that touch are an m for Tesseract at every size and with every language file, and it is sure of the m, so
    no rule for a word that it is not sure of mends it. A word of three letters or more that the list lacks becomes
    the list's word with "rn" where the m was read, where the list holds one such word. The census of the stored
    rows named five tokens on LitChoir's pages ("bom" four times, and "up-bome", which is "upborne"). 1 syllable
    gained on the clean copies, 3 on all scan pages, none lost; no word of the fresh set changes.
  - **A slur that touches a word, and a dash that is a piece of a bow** (`ocr.BOW`, a rule for one print in what
    it trims). carol-great-rejoicing sets the slurs of the lower staff into its rows of words, and Tesseract read
    letters from them: "the" with a slur behind it is "thes", "a" is "avs", "in" is "inn.". A bow is one thin
    stroke in each column, over a row height or more, whose middle rises or falls; a level stroke is a hyphen or
    the line of a held syllable. A word that the list lacks and that Tesseract is not sure of, with such a bow
    behind its letters, is its letters before the bow where they are a word of the list. The first form trimmed 30
    words on LitChoir's scan pages and 5 of them rightly: the hook of a clef by hand, a hyphen over a slur, a slur
    alone and the tail of a letter by hand had been read as short words, and the trim made words of one letter of
    them. Measured at each of the 30 places, the ink before the bow tells: true letters fill the rows of their row
    (0.84 of them or more against 0.61 at most), and as many stand there as were read, by the usual width of a
    letter of the row (within 0.15 of a letter against 0.49 or more). The five that are left are three words of
    the carol and two of two copies by hand ("O" and "sit"). The second half of the rule: a dash that would join
    two words as a hyphen does is no hyphen where a bow runs through its middle. Tesseract gives one box to a true
    hyphen and the piece of a slur that comes close to it: of the 114 dashes that the first form dropped, 57 hold
    other ink in their box, and 10 of the 16 of those that were looked at hold a true hyphen. A dash goes only
    where its box holds nothing but the bow. What the rule takes out of a row is taken out for the test that sets
    the readings of a row beside each other too (`ocr.MEND_ALIKE`): that test had found "praisingthes" at every
    size and the mending then left it. Clean copies 2 gained and none lost; all scan pages 4 and none; no word of
    the fresh set changes. The rule acts at 62 places of the stored rows (5 words, 57 dashes) and at 27 more in the
    full pass, on the pages whose stored rows are older than the page (see below). Each was looked at on the page:
    every dash that went is a piece of a slur, of a tie or of a fermata.
  - **An open word that the list parts so is not mended** (`ocr.MEND_HEAD`). One dash that the first form of the
    slur rule dropped was a true hyphen, and its loss had made a word right: nativity-kontakion ends a row with
    "shep-", the mending made "she-" of it, and without the hyphen it stayed "shep". The step that lost the word
    was the mending. A word that a hyphen leaves open at the end of a row is the beginning of a word, and the list
    is asked how often it parts a word after these letters ("shep" of "shep-herds", "cher-u" of "cher-u-bim"), not
    whether it holds them as a word. Such a word is not mended, and no word of another reading takes its place,
    unless the other word begins the list's parted words more often: holding back every such piece lost "up-",
    which was read "op-" ("op" begins a parted word 3 times in the list, "up" 85 times). A credit for such a piece
    in the choice among the readings of a row gained 10 syllables and lost 7 on all scan pages, a border that
    moves, and is not in the rule. Clean copies 1 gained and none lost; all scan pages 7 and none
    (cherubic-kastorsky and its hierarchical scan "cher-u-", pascha-come-take-light--s2 "glo-ri-"); the fresh set 1
    and none.
  - The stored rows of the quick count are older than the page on 39 scan pages of LitChoir. They are a census of
    16 November, and those pages were built anew since: their rows stand at other rows of the picture now, by 88
    pixels on one page. A rule of the quick count that looks at the ink looked at the music over the row
    there and seemed to drop a true hyphen. The quick count leaves the slur rule off on those pages (a list with
    the wide test, outside git; none on the fresh set), and the full pass, which wrote down every place where the
    rule acts, showed them. A new census of the rows of these pages is due.
  - Looked at, no rule. nativity-canon-ode-9 lacks an "in" under a loop in pencil: every reading holds a lone "i"
    in a box as wide as two letters there. A census of the 87 lone small i of LitChoir's rows showed about 18 such
    wide boxes, most of them stray ink at the start of a row: no rule. wedding-alleluia-grechaninov lacks one syllable
    that only the credit for an open word had found. All seven rules together, by the quick count: 11 syllables
    gained and none lost on the clean copies, 39 and 4 on all scan pages, 43 and 1 on the fresh set.
  - The full reading with the words, every scan page with Tesseract (`w376v1` against `w375v1`; the lists by the
    page and the scorer are as in round 82): no note, no layout and no window of any score differs. Clean copies
    22,088 of 22,649 notes (97.5 %; 40 at 99 %; 21,956 of 22,605 as typed), as before; 6,080 of 6,254 syllables
    (97.2 %; 6,016 of 6,259 as typed) and 4,310 of 4,527 whole words (95.2 %): 11 syllables gained and none lost
    (carol-great-rejoicing 4, memorial-troparia--s2 3, nativity-kontakion, liturgy-we-have-seen, pascha-hypakoe
    and its second scan 1 each), 10 whole words gained and 1 lost. The base scored as typed gives the figures of
    round 82 exactly (21,956 of 22,605 and 6,005 of 6,259). 37 clean scores read every printed note (556 lacking),
    43 every syllable (174 lacking; 39 and 185 before), 31 both (29), 39 have no note that is not there (217 with
    the places that a hand changed apart, 222 without) and 31 are whole in all three (29). 44 clean scores have
    99 % of their syllables (40), and 158 syllables are lacking for 99 % of each (168). The whole word that a
    clean score lost is an "Al-le-lu-ia" of cherubic-lvovsky that goes on over the end of a system. Its hyphens
    stand under a slur that runs into the bar line, and Tesseract read them with the slur as "=———=—+", which is
    no hyphen for the reader because of the +; the hyphen after "lu" had been read from a piece of the slur, which
    the slur rule takes now. The word was right by a wrong reading: the rule stays, and a hyphen that is read with
    a stray sign is on the list of what is left (a first count of it from stored rows: 6 syllables gained and none
    lost on all scan pages). In a row of that score that the count does not take, the same rule takes a piece of a
    slur that had joined two stray words at the end of the row, and a reading with "CHE-RU-B-IM" then wins over
    the reading with "CHE-RU-BIM": the stray words are a slur that was read as a word, which is left too. All
    scores 78,705 of 89,863 notes and 2,515 that are not there, as before; syllables 25,599 of 28,695 (89.2 %;
    25,413 of 28,700 as typed), 42 gained and 4 lost, and whole words 16,905 of 20,590, 34 gained and 8 lost. The
    full pass gains 3 syllables more than the quick count, which reads one row for each staff and leaves the slur
    rule off on 39 pages. The four lost are on the three scores that the quick count named (liturgy-first-antiphon 2, pascha-canon-ode-6 1,
    st-basil-champion-leader--s2 1). Scans 19,946 of 22,590 syllables (88.3 %) and 13,976 of 16,241 whole words;
    thick copies 1,771 of 1,980 and 1,207 of 1,371; copies by hand 12,095 of 14,356 and 8,459 of 10,343. The kit:
    6,232 of 6,901 by parts and 5,800 of 6,419 staff by staff, as before. The fresh set, every page read with the
    words (`fr376` against `fr375`): 39,957 of 41,761 notes (95.7 %) and 630 that are not there, as before; 8,344
    of 11,341 syllables (73.6 %), 43 gained and 1 lost, and 5,917 of 6,647 whole words (89.0 %), 27 gained and 9
    lost (7 at a hyphen that was read as a stop, see above, and 2 with the lost syllable). The repository's code
    was read against the measured copy by the quick count on all scan pages and on the fresh set: no word differs.
- Round 82 (21 November 2026): a wedge of paper at a staff line, the faint line that a scanner draws down a page, the
  stroke of an engraved flag, a word that goes on over the end of a page, and a 1 that is an i.

  The round stayed with the clean scores that are nearly whole: those that lack one to three notes, and those that
  read every printed note and still lack a syllable or read a note that is not there. Eleven of them were looked at
  place by place again, with the hymn's other scans. Three rules for the notes and two for the words came of it. The
  first rule is a rule for every copy; the scanner's line is a rule for one scan and the flag a rule for one print of
  LitChoir, and each of these two was narrowed on LitChoir's own pages after its first form did more than it was
  written for. cherubic-georgian, which lacked three notes and a syllable and read a note that is not there, is whole
  now.

  - **A wedge of paper between a staff line, an upright stroke and a slanted one is no head** (`symbols.FLAT_WEDGE`).
    Four clean scores read a note that is not there at such a place: a slanted beam at a stem (cherubic-georgian--s2),
    a slur at a bar line (memorial-troparia--s2), the arc of a pencil ring at a stem (liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2) and
    a line by pen across a bar line (nativity-kontakion). The three strokes close in a small cell that is level along
    the staff line, and it was read as the inside of a half note or a whole note. `symbols.FLAT_CELL` (round 52)
    refuses such a cell from 0.6 staff spaces of height on; a lower one can be the half of an inside that a staff line
    cuts off a head on the line. The two differ in their shape and in what lies across the line. The cell is a wedge:
    at one end it is as high as it gets (the mean of an eighth of its columns is 0.85 of its highest column or more),
    at the other it is a point (0.35 at most), and its highest column stands within a tenth of its width of an end.
    And the paper that mirrors it across the line is open, in 0.7 of it or more: the half of a head has the other half
    of the head there, ink or closed paper. The census was taken at the reader's own step on every scan page of
    LitChoir: 3,250 low cells that are level at a line, 93 of them such wedges in a mark that is no fatter than
    `FLAT_CELL` allows. At the 58 true heads among them, which a hand drew as a loop from the stem on a line, the
    paper across the line is open in 0.47 at most; at the others in 0.54 or more. The 24 from 0.7 on were each looked
    at: cells at beams, slurs, flags, a pencil ring, a sharp and strokes by hand, and no head. The rule's own code
    acts on one page more than the census named (a cell at a slanted beam on a copy by hand, a note that is not
    there). Alone, on the pages of the round: 16 pages differ and 17 marks go, each looked at and none a note. By the
    count 1 note is gained and 4 are lost, and 17 notes that are not there go, 4 of them on the clean copies
    (cherubic-georgian--s2 3 to 2, liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 2 to 1, memorial-troparia--s2 5 to 4, nativity-kontakion
    4 to 3). On two scores a mark goes and no count moves (liturgy-litanies, transfiguration-antiphon). The gain is
    thomas-koinonikon (146 to 147 of 174): a whole note that is not there goes, and the whole note beside it is lined
    up right. The loss is the count's, on lamentations-canon-ode-5, a copy by hand (68 to 64 of 108): its ison's whole
    note stands between two brackets by hand, the cell between the first bracket, the bar line and a staff line goes,
    and the whole note then stands in one column with the half note that is still read from the second bracket. It
    gets the second voice there, and the count's split of the staff into melody and ison then gives four notes of the
    melody to the ison. Every mark that the rule took is no note: the rule stays as it is, and the second bracket is
    on the list of what is left. The fresh set, every page: 54 pages differ, 18 notes gained and 1 lost, and 138 notes
    that are not there go (786 to 648). Of the 164 read notes that go, 24 taken at random were looked at: each is the
    cell that the flag of a note with its stem down closes with the stem and a staff line. The lost note is an eighth
    note whose flag is not read. It had been made short by the sum of its bar, with half notes in it that are not
    there (cells between the flags of sixteenths) and are gone now; it is a quarter note again. A reading that was
    right by a wrong count: no rule is narrowed for it, and the flag is on the list of what is left.
  - **The faint line that a scanner draws down a page is no stem** (`layers.SCAN_LINE`, a rule for one scan).
    cherubic-georgian lacked a half note of the alto. A faint line runs down two pages of its scan from top to bottom,
    as a speck on a scanner's glass draws it, and on one page it runs up along the left edge of that head. A mark of
    the size of a head with a stroke at each side that goes the same way is a piece of a beam for the reader, so the
    head was no head; on the next page a note that is not there was read at the line. `layers.STRAY` (round 49) takes
    a line that passes a system at both ends where it is an unbroken column of ink; this line is faint and leans, so
    it is none. It is looked for in the grey picture: an upright stroke that is ink or 20 grey levels darker than the
    paper half a staff space beside it, in 0.9 of the rows from over the system to under it and in 0.9 of the rows of
    each end past the staves, along a line that leans by at most 0.012 columns in a row, and no wider than `STRAY`
    allows. The census is every such stroke of LitChoir's scan pages and of the fresh set: 1,121 candidates on
    LitChoir, 119 that pass a system at both ends, 41 of them thin, on 20 pages, each looked at. They are lines
    through a whole page (six pages), the upright strokes of clefs on systems of one staff (seven pages), a pencil
    ring, a long curve by hand, the shadow of a page's edge and the edges in a photograph. A clef's stroke passes a
    system of one staff at both ends, so the rule is kept to systems of two staves or more, and to lines one staff
    space or more inside both ends of the staves, where the line that opens a system and its last bar line stand. Nine
    thin lines are left that `STRAY` does not take: five are the scanner's line on the two pages of cherubic-georgian,
    two are the opening line of a system in the shadow of the page's edge, and two stand in a photograph. The first
    form took a true stem with the line: on the second page the stem of a quarter note stands exactly on it. A stem is
    black and wider, so a row's piece is taken only where its darkness, summed, is at most 1.8 times the median of the
    line's pieces (322 to 364 in a row for the line, 369 to 559 beside the half note, and 687 to 1,165 for the stem).
    Alone: 2 pages differ, the half note is read (cherubic-georgian 479 to 480 of 482) and the note that is not there
    is gone (1 to 0). On two more pages the rule takes a few pixels and no note changes. On the fresh set it finds no
    line.
  - **The stroke of an engraved flag is no stem** (`symbols.FLAG_STROKE`, a rule for one print of LitChoir).
    cherubic-georgian lacked two more notes, both in one staff of the bass. Its print draws the flag of a stem that
    goes down as a wedge at the stem with a thin stroke that comes back up to the head. The stroke was read as a stem,
    with the wedge as a head at its end (`symbols.thin_heads`), and the two eighth notes that are not there took the
    places of two true notes in the count. A head that was read from its stem is such a flag where the stem ends in
    the box of another head, that head's own stem comes the other way and goes on past the mark by half a staff space
    or more, the mark stands between the two strokes, and a flag was counted at that stem on its side, within half a
    staff space of the mark's row. The census was made from the stored notes of LitChoir's scan pages: of 31 heads
    read from a stem beside a flagged stem, 11 have their own stem end at the other head, and the true notes among
    them stand at the end of the other stem, where the rule does not look. The first form also took a scribble by hand
    on a page of megalynarion-bortniansky--s2, two heads read from stems that end in each other (no count moved). A
    first narrowing asked for a stroke of two staff spaces, a limit set between the two cases of LitChoir (3.1 and
    1.1): on the fresh set the rule then stopped at every one of the eight pages where its first form had acted. A
    limit that is set between two cases is the wrong measure. What LitChoir's own page shows is the shape: the flag
    hangs on the stem of a true head, so the other head must be a head by its own ink and not one read from a stem. So
    narrowed, alone: one page of LitChoir differs, 2 notes gained and none lost (cherubic-georgian 479 to 481), and
    the scribble reads as on main. The fresh set, whose engravings draw their flags so too: 8 pages differ, 1 note
    gained and none lost, and 18 notes that are not there go (786 to 768), each the flag of an eighth note with its
    stem down, looked at.
  - The three rules together, every scan page (`n375` against `n372`): 18 pages differ, 4 notes gained and 4 lost by
    parts (3 and none on the clean copies), 6 and 5 staff by staff (3 and none). The key, the clefs and the time
    signatures of the changed pages are as on main. One layout moves (pentecost-koinonikon, a copy by hand: no count
    by parts, one note more staff by staff) and two windows (carol-heaven-and-earth--s2, no count by parts and one
    note fewer staff by staff, and liturgy-litanies, no count); no count of the notes that no page prints moves. No
    rule of the music step changed, so the software-made PDFs were not read anew. The fresh set, every page (`fn375`
    against `fn372`): 61 pages differ, 19 notes gained and 1 lost, 156 notes that are not there fewer (786 to 630),
    and 101 of its 214 pieces at 99 % (99).
  - **A word that goes on over the end of a page** (`ocr.MEND_PAGE`). cherubic-georgian lacked one syllable, the last
    of an "Al-le-lu-ia" whose first half ends one page and whose second half begins the next. Since round 70 the first
    row under a part's staff is handed the word that the row before left open (`ocr.MEND_BEFORE`), from system to
    system of one page. Now the hymn hands it over the page too: `read_rows` takes the open words of the page before
    and leaves its own for the next (`cli.set_words`). By the quick count from stored rows, with the rule off against
    all rules on: 4 syllables gained and none lost on the clean copies, 5 and none on all scan pages, none either way
    on the fresh set. It changes seven rows, each looked at: the second half of the alleluia in cherubic-georgian and
    in two rows of its second scan (one of the two keeps a stray piece), "re-" and "pose" (memorial-troparia), "para-"
    and "dise", which was read "kiss" (st-basil-in-thee-rejoiceth), one stray piece that goes
    (wedding-alleluia-grechaninov), and a capital that is small now (liturgy-only-begotten).
  - **A 1 that is an i** (`ocr.DIGIT_LIST`). The row at the top of that page was read "lu - 1a.": Tesseract read the i
    as a 1. A 1 among small letters was an l (`ocr.DIGITS`), and the mending with the open word did not act, because
    it mends only a word that Tesseract is not sure of, and Tesseract was sure of the digit. The first form counted
    such a piece as one that Tesseract is not sure of where the list holds the word with neither letter, so that the
    mending may choose. Short pieces that had been read right then went with it (the "ly" of "ho-ly", read "1y"), and
    with them the reading of their row: 2 syllables gained and 4 lost on all scan pages by the quick count. The list
    of words tells it directly now: the 1 is an i where the list holds the word with an i and not with an l. The word
    is the piece with the pieces that its hyphens join it to, with the open word of the row before where the piece
    stands in the first word of its row, or the piece alone; the piece stays as sure as Tesseract was. By the quick
    count, with the rule off against all rules on: 1 syllable gained and none lost on the clean copies, 3 and none on
    all scan pages; no word of the fresh set changes. Without the rule for the end of a page it gains 2 on all scan
    pages and none on the clean copies: the alleluia needs both. Six words change on all scan pages, each looked at.
    Four are right now ("alleluia", "today is salvation", "the Lord is God", and "who is thus well pleased", which the
    page prints where the hymn is typed "who art"), and two were wrong and are wrong otherwise: a "lin-" that was read
    with a quote mark for its l, and a stray piece at the start of a row that was read "an" and is read "is".
  - Looked at, no rule. The single short syllables that the clean copies lack ("in", "o", "a", "to", about 35) were
    traced through the stored readings of their rows: no reading of any size or file holds them, so no rule that
    chooses among the readings can bring them (nativity-canon-ode-9 lacks an "in" under a loop in pencil).
    carol-great-rejoicing lacks four syllables by misread letters. liturgy-we-praise-thee--s2 still reads one note
    that is not there, a cell at a paperclip. cherubic-georgian--s2 still lacks three notes and reads two that are not
    there: a cell at a beam that is no wedge, a tie that runs into a slur, and a whole note; its scan has no line of a
    scanner. pascha-christ-is-risen-romanian lacks one note, on a page with three causes of its own.
    trisagion-obleschuk has half notes whose rings a pale print leaves open, wider than `symbols.THIN_RING` closes,
    and nativity-god-is-with-us five different causes.
  - The full reading with the words, every scan page with Tesseract (`w375v1` against `w372v1`; the lists by the page

- Round 81 (21 November 2026): a half note whose ring is open, a pencil ring that was read as a ledger line, and
  the two notes that are not there in one clean score.

  The round stayed with the clean scores that lack one to three notes or read one or two that are not there. Each
  place was looked at on the page again, with the hymn's other scans. Two places are matters of the count, and four
  rules came of the others. Two of the four are rules for one case; each was narrowed on LitChoir's own pages
  after its first form did more than it was written for.

  - **A slip that a hand pasted over the print** (the list of the notes that a hand changed).
    liturgy-mercy-of-peace lacked two notes, and one of them stands in the first system of one of its pages
    ("And to thy spirit", two staves, five chords). That system is a slip of paper that a hand wrote in pen and
    pasted over the print: the clef, the key, the heads, the stems and the words are the hand's, and the printed
    systems under it are another type and another ink (seen at four times the size and in the scan itself). Its
    20 notes count apart, as the notes of a page that a hand wrote whole do (round 62), and an entry of that
    list can now name one system of a page. The reader has 19 of the 20. The list holds 24 notes on three clean
    scores now, and the page by hand of a fourth.
  - **A printed note that is typed a step low** (the list of the notes by the page, 129 notes now).
    megalynarion-zakkak lacked three notes. One is a note of the bass inside a pencil ring, read as C3 and typed
    B2. The head stands in the second space, exactly at the row of the C3 before it, and it stands so in the
    hymn's second scan too; at four times the size it is unmistakable in both, and the notes before and after it
    line up as right. The ring is drawn around the two heads, and the heads are the print's: the note counts as
    the page prints it (354 to 355 of 357).
  - With the two lists, the reading of round 80 has 22,081 of 22,649 notes on the clean copies, 22,062 of 22,625
    printed notes with the places by hand apart (563 lacking), 19 of 24 notes by hand, and 225 notes that are not
    there; 34 scores read every printed note, as before. The rules below are compared with that.
  - **A half note whose ring is open** (`symbols.THIN_RING`, `symbols.ring_heads`).
    pascha-christ-is-risen-appalachian lacked two notes, one head that both voices of its staff sing: a faint
    dotted half note. The pale print left its ring open in two places, so no inside is closed, and the head was
    read from its stem: the arc of the ring at the stem's end was taken for the whole head, a filled head a step
    low (F sharp, a quarter note). The paper that the thin ink and the stems close in around such a piece, when
    breaks of 0.35 staff spaces are closed, is asked for the shape of the inside of a printed half note: 0.2
    staff spaces squared or more, a part of it still paper after the closing, 0.6 to 1.2 staff spaces wide and
    high and about as wide as high, 0.85 of its hull, and ink at 0.85 of its rim. The head is then hollow and
    stands where the ring stands. The census is every head that reaches the notes from a stem on LitChoir's scan
    pages: 3,189 on 316 pages, 2,788 of them filled with no inside. Six have such a ring. Five are lined up with
    a typed half note or dotted half note and none with a typed quarter or eighth note; the sixth is a note that
    is not there (letters by hand under a staff), and there the head of the ring is refused by the tests that
    every head from a stem passes, so the note stays as it was read.

    The first form made the head hollow where the stem's piece is found. Read on every scan page it changed three
    pages more than the census named, and the fresh set showed two notes that are not there. On those three
    pages (one of a clean copy, and one page that two hymns share) a head had been read from its inside by the
    step for hollow heads (`hole_heads`), which takes the arc at the stem as a piece of the rim; the hollow head
    from the stem stood in that step's way and took the head's place, and on the clean page the head kept one
    of its two stems and one of its two voices. No count moved by it. And the head of the ring went through the
    tests of a head from a stem in the place of the piece's own head: where the ring's head fails them a note
    went (the letters by hand, a note that is not there), and where only the piece's head fails them a note
    came (two new notes on the fresh set, and 2 notes lost in one piece by the line-up). The census had seen
    none of this, because it holds the heads that reach the notes. The rule now acts where its census was taken:
    the head is made as before, the head of the ring is kept beside it, and it takes the head's place after the
    hollow heads were read from their insides, where no hollow head stands at the ring. The narrowing came from
    the three pages of LitChoir. Alone, on the nine pages of the first form: 5 pages differ, 3
    notes gained and none lost by parts (pascha-christ-is-risen-appalachian 326 to 328 of 328;
    memorial-troparia 380 to 381 of 386, a dotted half note that was read a step low as a quarter note), 3 and
    none staff by staff (the third on liturgy-gospel-alleluia--s2, a copy by hand, whose count by parts does not
    move). In two scans of transfiguration-troparion the lower of two dotted half notes at one stem is such a
    head: it is at its pitch now and is a half note, but its dot is drawn out like a drip and is not read, so
    the note still counts as wrong. The fresh set, every page: one page differs. A letter of a heading over a staff, which
    was read as a quarter note before the round too, is a half note now, and the count shows it as one more note
    that is not there (3 to 4 in its piece); no note is gained or lost.
  - **The arc of a pencil ring under a head is no ledger line** (`symbols.LEDGER_TWIN`).
    liturgy-mercy-of-peace lacked one printed note, a C sharp on the first ledger line under the staff, read as
    B. A pencil ring is drawn around the head, and its arc lies level under the ledger line, less than half a
    staff space from it. Two such rows were one ledger line, at their mean: a thick line or one that leans gives
    two rows too. They are two strokes where paper parts them: 0.3 staff spaces apart or more, with three rows
    of paper between them at both sides of the head. The ledger line is then the one that stands nearer to a
    whole number of staff spaces from the staff. The census, at the reader's own step, is every head with two
    such rows on LitChoir's scan pages: 105 heads, 26 of them parted by paper. The first form dropped the other
    row of every parted pair: 5 pages differ, 3 notes gained and 1 lost. The lost one is a head by hand that is
    read from its upper half; its pitch had been right only by the mean of the two rows, one of them the head's
    own rim. A row that lies inside the head's box is the head's own ink and is left to the older rules: so
    narrowed, two heads of the census are left, the printed head in the pencil ring and a half note by hand
    whose upper rim stands apart. Alone, on the five pages: 2 pages differ, 2 notes gained and none lost by
    parts and staff by staff (liturgy-mercy-of-peace 400 to 401 of 402, with the notes by hand apart 382 of
    382, and the count no longer shows a note that is not there in that score, 1 to 0;
    resurrection-troparion-6--s2, a copy by hand, 153 to 154). The fresh set has no head with two such rows.
  - **Three half notes on lines, cut into five heads** (`symbols.LINE_CHORD_ENDS`, a rule for one case).
    trisagion-vitoshinsky reads every note and had two that are not there. One stood in its last chord: three
    half notes that touch, each on a staff line. The lines cut their insides into cells, and the heads are
    counted from the cells that are a quarter as large as the largest; a smudge at the lowest head left the last
    cell a hair under that, the chord was not read as heads on lines, and five heads were cut where three
    stand. The first and the last cell hold half an inside each and the cells between them two halves, so the
    end cells count twice. With three cells (two heads) that count changes what 119 marks of LitChoir's scan
    pages give, pairs of half notes that are read today among them: it is made only from four cells on. There
    it changes 17 marks: 15
    clefs and the numbers of one time signature, which give no heads either way, and the chord. Alone, on the
    twelve pages of those marks: one page differs, the chord is three heads at their pitches, and the note that
    is not there is gone (2 to 1). On the fresh set no mark of the census has four cells.
  - **The middle of a bold slur is no head** (`symbols.BOW_MIDDLE`, a rule for one case). The other note of
    trisagion-vitoshinsky that is not there: the slur under two quarter notes swells in its middle, and the
    middle was read as a filled head with one arm of the bow as its stem. A first census, from the stored
    notes, measured the other arm: of 422 long, slim filled marks with one stem, one had it. The rule written
    from it, with its own arithmetic, took three notes on the 66 pages of that census, and two of them are true:
    a head on a ledger dash with its stem in its middle, and a head by hand with its flag. The stored notes
    keep a mark's middle and its size, and the rule measures the head's box. A second census at the reader's own
    step (965 wide filled marks with one stem on the scan pages, 26 of them as wide, as slim and with as short a
    stem as the rule asks, 5 with an arm) showed what parts the bow: its stem stands at one end of the mark
    (0.11 and 0.12 of its width from the end, on this slur and on a bow under two notes that a later step
    takes), and the stems of the heads and of a fermata stand in the mark (0.39 to 0.48). A bow has one arm at
    each end of its middle. Alone, on the pages of the five: one page differs, the one note is gone (2 to 1).
    With both rules trisagion-vitoshinsky has no note that is not there and is whole. The fresh set, every page
    with the four rules: no page differs by this rule.
  - Looked at, no rule. megalynarion-zakkak still lacks two: a dotted half note under a slur in pencil that is
    read as a tie to the note at the slur's other end, and a quarter note that touches the beamed head of the
