    19,904 of 22,590 syllables; thick copies 7,082 of 8,532; copies by hand 29,498 of 38,519 and 12,075 of
    14,356. The kit: 6,229 of 6,901 by parts and 5,799 of 6,419 staff by staff, as before. The fresh set: no
    page differs by either rule (39,939 of 41,761 notes, 8,302 of 11,341 syllables, 5,899 of 6,647 whole
    words).

- Round 76 (20 November 2026): a row that goes on from an open word, a stray small i, one read piece under one
  note, and a cell between two staff lines before a head.

  The round went on with the clean scores that are nearly whole: those that lack one to three notes or a few
  syllables. Each was looked at on the page, and what a score lacks was traced through the readings of its row
  or the marks of its staff. Three rules for the words and one for the notes came of it, and a tool that makes
  a full reading of the words cheap. Most of what the scores lack had no rule; it is listed at the end.

  - **A cache of Tesseract's lines** (a tool outside git, with the wide test). What Tesseract reads of a line
    depends only on the picture of the line, the size and the language file. The wide test keeps each such
    reading by the picture of its line now. A pass of every scan page with main's code filled the cache (31,018
    lines; 80 minutes) and scored exactly as the reading before the round. After it a full reading of the 485
    scan pages with the words costs only the reader's own time, about half an hour with the scoring. A rule of
    the words that asks the notes, which the replay from stored rows cannot count, is counted exactly on every
    page so, and such a rule off alone is one more pass. The fresh set has its cache too: read from it with
    main's code it scores as before (8,300 of 11,341 syllables, 5,899 of 6,647 whole words).
  - **A row that goes on from the word that the row before leaves open** (`ocr.MEND_REST`, `MEND_REST_FIT`,
    `MEND_REST_STRAY`). Round 70 hands a row the open letters of the row before it, and the first word of the
    row is the rest of that word where the two make a word of the list. Three cases were left. The rest is
    parted further ("car-" at the end of a row, and the next row begins "ry-ing"): the first syllables make
    the word of the list with the open piece, and the word was mended alone, to "crying". Such a word is not
    mended now. The letters of a first word that goes on so count in the fit of its reading as the letters of
    a word of the list do: a reading at another size that began with a word of the list ("Ty-ing") was as good
    a fit and won by how sure Tesseract was. And a short word before the word that goes on, of which Tesseract
    is not sure, is stray ink at the start of the row, the brace, a bar line or the clef ("pas-", and the next
    row begins "he cha"). Counted by the replay with all three: 5 syllables gained on all scan pages and none
    lost, about 30 false short words fewer, and no syllable gained or lost on the fresh set. Each part gains
    alone: with one part off the replay loses 1 (the word that is not mended), 4 (the fit), 2 (the fit for a
    first word that is the whole rest) and 1 (the stray word).
  - **A small i that stands alone** (`ocr.SMALL_I`). The word I is a capital. The brace, the clef and a bar
    line beside a row are read as a small i that stands alone: 61 such words on LitChoir's scan pages, none of
    them a typed word, and 4 on the fresh set (one of them a piece of "Si-na-i", which was read "sin a i" and
    is not right with it or without it). The first form took the i out of each reading of a row, and gained 1
    syllable and lost 8: a reading without its stray word was whole, the other sizes were not read, and a
    syllable beside the word stayed lost, as round 53 found for the stray word under a clef. The word goes
    after the reading of the row is chosen now: no syllable gained or lost on all scan pages and on the fresh
    set, and the 61 false words are gone. The false words that this rule and the rule before take away had
    stood between true words in the line-up of the count: the full reading has 38 whole words more on all
    scores and 2 fewer. The 2 are the count's. On one score a stray i at the start of a row had stood for the
    word I, whose true reading carries a false hyphen, and on another the line-up moved where two false
    words went. No syllable changed on either.
  - **Two syllables of one read piece under one note** (`lyrics.ONE_NOTE`, with the mark `cut` of `ocr._parted`).
    The list of words parts a word that the page prints whole ("glorious" into "glo-ri-ous"), because a scan
    loses a hyphen far more often than a page sets two syllables under one note. Where the page prints
    "glo-rious" under two notes the reader then wrote three syllables, one of them under no note, and the
    typed hymn has two. Two syllables that the list made of one read piece, with one note for the two, between
    syllables with notes of their own, are one syllable now, where the piece stands under that note as one
    syllable does. The rule had three forms. The first asked only for a syllable without a note beside its
    neighbour of the same word, and gained 6 syllables and lost 9 on all scan pages: it joined a letter that
    the mending had added to the word after it ("a-pos", "e-ter", where the first letter has no ink), and two
    pieces that were read apart where the note of one was not read ("ma-ni"). The second set the mark only
    where both syllables are letters of exactly one piece of the page as it was read, which the mending did
    not change and which gives no letters to a third syllable. Counted by a full reading with the rule off,
    it gained 8 syllables on all scan pages and lost none, and each of its 18 places was looked at: the page
    prints the piece whole at each. The fresh set then gained 2 syllables and lost 7. Its engravings print a
    word whole under two notes where the syllables stand close ("translate", "after", "giving"), and where
    the reader lacks one of the two notes (a note of the melody that touches the ison's breve under it) the
    two true syllables were joined. The third form was set on LitChoir's 18 places: at the true ones the
    middle of the piece stands within half a staff space of its note, and a note that stands over one
    syllable of a longer word stands beside the middle of the piece. The piece's middle must stand in the
    columns of the note's head (0.65 staff spaces). A piece that begins under its note, as a held syllable
    does, is left out with it (2 of LitChoir's 8 counted places, "glo-rious" on a copy by hand and "blessed"
    on a clean copy): a first syllable of one letter begins under its note too, and LitChoir's pages do not
    show enough such places to part them. The third form: 6 syllables gained on all scan pages and none
    lost, 3 of them on the clean copies ("O-ri-ent" on two scores, "glo-ri-ous", "hal-low-ed" on two scans,
    "i-a"); the fresh set 2 gained and none lost.
  - **A cell between two staff lines before a head** (`symbols.CELL_BETWEEN`). A sharp or a bar line close
    before a head closes a cell of paper with the head and the two staff lines, and the piece was read as one
    hollow mark. Round 53 takes such a cell out where it lies along one line, with a thin stroke at one side
    and a head at the other. A cell that fills the whole room between two lines is level along both, and was
    not taken. The first form took it with the thin stroke at either side, changed 68 pages, and gained 11
    notes and lost 4: a flag closes such a cell with its own head, older rules read the flag from it, and two
    eighth notes of a clean copy became quarters. A flag hangs at the right of its stem, so the second form
    asked for the thin stroke at the left: 13 pages, 10 notes gained and 2 lost on LitChoir. The fresh set
    showed what LitChoir's pages do not: the flag of the note before closes the same cell with the next head,
    and one eighth note lost its flag there (the count stayed level). The third form was set on LitChoir's own
    cells, the 103 of the census. Three measures did not part a flag from a bar line or a sign: a stroke that
    goes on past both lines (a bar line in the bottom space ends at the staff), the share of the staff's rows
    that the stroke fills (the stroke of the clean page is a sharp, 0.8 of them), and how straight the edge of
    the cell is (the arc of a pencil ring 0.08 staff spaces, the belly of a flag 0.05). How far the stroke
    goes on upright does: the bar lines 3.8 staff spaces or more, the stroke of the sharp 3.1 and a pencil
    ring beside its head 3.2, and the flags of LitChoir's pages 1.3, 1.3 and 2.3. The limit is 2.7. Alone, by
    the reading of the notes: 11 pages differ, 10 notes gained on all scores and 2 lost, 3 gained on the clean
    copies and none lost, and no page of the fresh set differs. The clean gain is one score (triodion-meatfare,
    178 to 181 of 196, a sharp close before a chord, and one note that is not there fewer); on another clean
    score a cell beside a pencil ring was read as a note that is not there, and is gone (nativity-canon-ode-5,
    2 to 1). The 2 lost are two scans of one print (ascension-antiphon): one false eighth note went on each,
    no other note of the page changed, and the count's layout turned from two staves to four (4 notes that
    are not there fewer on each). On one copy the lowest whole note of a stack close after a bar line is
    read now, and the count shows 2 notes that are not there more (liturgy-second-antiphon, 17 to 19); on
    another copy 2 notes are gained and 1 more is not there. Two cells of the second form are left out by the
    limit: one at the stem of the note before (1.8) and one at a sign.
  - **Looked at, no rule.** nativity-canon-ode-1 lacks only "our God", printed words that a hand crossed
    out: how such words count is the owner's open question, and the count stays as it is. nativity-canon-ode-9
    lacks "in" under a pencil ring, which Tesseract reads "i" at every size. The arc of the ring on
    nativity-canon-ode-5 is also read as a stem with a small head, the one note that is not there on that
    score. Heads that are read from their stems (`symbols.thin_heads`): 3,017 on all scan scores and 157 on
    the clean copies, 32 of them not there and 20 at a wrong pitch; no one shape parts the false ones. The
    flat ones are false on the clean copies (none right of 18) and true on the thick copies. A sign of a print
    was found and not used: the filled heads of a printed page are of one width (the spread is under 0.05 of
    the width on nine clean pages of ten, and 0.053 to 0.14 on pages by hand). pascha-receive-short lacks two
    worn half notes at a ledger line, a blot; cherubic-lvovsky (lacks 101) has hairline stems and beams that
    the scan lost; on liturgy-we-praise-thee the rule that makes the heads of a chord as long as each other
    cuts a printed dot; on megalynarion-kievan a cell stands between a head and a line at its right, which
    the rule leaves with the cells of flags.
  - The full reading of every page, with the words (`w356v1` against `w352v1`): clean copies 22,060 of 22,649
    notes (97.4 %; 40 at 99 %; 21,928 of 22,606 as typed), 3 gained and none lost; 6,067 of 6,254 syllables
    (97.0 %; 6,003 of 6,259 as typed), 5 gained and none lost; whole words 4,298 of 4,527, 4 gained and none
    lost; 39 scores at 99 % of their syllables (37); 456 notes that are not there (446 with the places that a
    hand changed apart), 2 fewer; staff by staff 4 gained and none lost. The base scored as typed gives the
    figures of round 75 exactly (21,925 of 22,606 and 5,998 of 6,259). All scores 78,655 of 89,866 (87.5 %; 94
    at 99 %): 10 gained and 2 lost by parts, 9 gained and 2 lost staff by staff, and 7 notes that are not
    there fewer (4,715). Syllables 25,557 of 28,695 (89.1 %), 13 gained and none lost: 6 by the read piece
    under one note and 7 by the rows that go on (5 by the replay). Whole words 16,874 of 20,590, 38 gained and
    2 lost. 30 clean scores read every printed note, 38 every syllable (36), 24 both (23), 27 have no note
    that is not there, and 21 are whole in all three (20; the new one is presentation-christ-apolytikion); 11
    lack one to three notes; 585 printed notes and 187 syllables are lacking, and 455 notes are needed for 99 %
    of each clean score. The fresh set: 39,939 of 41,761 notes as before (99 pieces at 99 %), and no page
    differs in its notes; 8,302 of 11,341 syllables, 2 gained and none lost, and 5,899 of 6,647 whole words
    as before; its reading with the words and its reading of every page agree piece by piece. The second
    form of the rule for one read piece had 8,295 there (2 gained and 7 lost), and the two other rules of the
    words alone are level (8,300). The kit: 6,229 by parts and 5,799 staff by staff, as before.

- Round 75 (20 November 2026): a bold key signature, the numbers of a time signature, the ison's breve under no
  letter, and a refrain that a page prints once more.

  The round began on the notes that are not there on the clean copies, 535 after round 74. They were set on
  sheets score by score, by the parts of the count, and the scores that hold most were looked at first. One
  cause was a matter of the count; four were marks that stand at the start of a staff or beside a bar line and
  were read as notes: the signs of a key, the numbers of a time signature and the breves of an ison. The fifth
  is one print's way to set a whole note. Each rule came from a census of every page of LitChoir with each mark
  looked at, the first forms were read on every page of LitChoir and of the fresh set, and every time signature
  and every note that they changed was set on a sheet and looked at. That narrowed two of the rules.

  - **A refrain that the page prints once more than the hymn is typed** (the scorer's relation `more`). The
    clean score with the most notes that are not there (wedding-psalm-128, 53) prints its refrain with all of
    its notes after the fourth verse. A hand wrote "NO REFRAIN" beside it with an arrow down to the fifth verse,
    and the hymn is typed without it. The count held such a page in a window, and the 53 notes of the refrain were
    notes that are not there. The page prints them: the stretch is told to the scorer with its place, as a
    stretch that a scan does not show is, and its notes and words count as the page prints them. The score has
    500 of 519 notes now (455 of 465 before) and 5 notes that are not there. The count of the clean copies
    grows by 54 notes and 10 syllables. The reading before the round, scored again with it, has 22,056 of
    22,649 notes on the clean copies, 6,061 of 6,254 syllables and 487 notes that are not there; as typed it
    gives the figures of round 74 exactly (21,924 of 22,606).
  - **A bold key signature** (`starts.KEY_PLACES`). Where the strokes of a heavy sharp ran together, where the
    signs touch each other or the clef, or where pieces of the clef pass as signs, the key signature was found
    too short, the notes of the staff began inside it, and its last signs were read as notes. The hymn's key
    and the staff's clef say where each sign of the key must stand. The marks after the clef are the key
    signature where each stands at its place and is as large as a sign, and the notes begin after them. The
    first form set the end of the key signature by these places wherever they fit, and moved the starts of 185
    staves by a census of every page (4,719 staves): a quarter rest after a flat stands as a flat does, and the
    dots of a bass clef stand at the place of its first sharp. The rule is a guard now. It acts only where the
    notes would begin before the last sign of the key, and only moves their start later: 21 staves on 18
    pages, each looked at. Alone: 2 notes gained on all scores and none lost, none on the clean copies, and 12
    notes that are not there fewer on three clean scores; 6 pages differ in their notes; no page of the fresh
    set differs. On a copy by hand a flat of the key had been read as a note, and it made the next notes of
    its pitch flat: one of them is right now.
  - **The numbers of a time signature that stand short of their lines** (`meter.TIME_LOOSE`). The finder asks
    that a number begins and ends within 0.2 staff spaces of the top, the middle or the bottom line. The
    numbers of some prints begin 0.3 away, were not found, and were read as notes. With 0.3 in the place of 0.2
    the finder read 44 true time signatures more and 8 false ones, and lost a true one. The false ones were
    signs of a key: two flats of a bass staff's key, one over the other, and a heavy sharp fill the two halves
    of a staff as two numbers do (and a note with a flag passed at the first look at a start, which reaches 13
    staff spaces past the clef). The lost one stood beside a sharp that passed the wider limit, was grouped
    with the numbers, and the group was no signature. The closer limit is read first now and keeps what it
    finds. The wider limit adds numbers in other columns, and at the start of a staff only after the key
    signature: of the 23 such signs at the starts of LitChoir's staves, each looked at, the 7 signs of a key
    begin 1.2 staff spaces or more before the end of the key signature, and the 16 time signatures 0.17 or
    more after it. Alone: 8 notes gained on all scores and 1 lost, none on the clean copies, and 10 notes that
    are not there fewer on four clean scores; 19 pages differ. One page of the fresh set differs: 3 notes that
    are not there fewer, no note gained or lost.
  - **Numbers beside the numbers of another staff** (`meter.TIME_MATE`). The staves of a system change the time
    at one place, after a bar line. Where one staff shows the numbers there by every measure, the ink of
    another staff in the same columns after its bar line, which fills the two halves of the staff as two
    numbers do, is the same signature: it had missed a check for a bar line, a head or a stem by a pixel (two
    numbers that touch). Alone such ink is no signature. Alone: 4 notes gained on other scans, none lost, and 4
    notes that are not there fewer on one clean score; 5 pages differ; no page of the fresh set.
  - **What the time signatures show.** No count shows a false time signature that takes no note. The stored
    pages of two runs were set beside each other, and each changed signature was looked at on a tile that is
    centred on its own staff: 53 in the first form, and after the narrowing 43 that are read and were not
    before, none that is gone and none that is false. One true signature that only the first form read is not
    read now; it changes no note.
  - **The ison's breve under no letter** (`drone.BREVE_BARE`). The rule of round 64 takes a breve that stands
    under a letter of the ison. One print also restates the ison at the start of a staff as a breve with no
    letter over it, and a breve under the melody's note, and both were read as whole notes. A breve has an
    upright stroke of its own at each side. Of the hollow heads without a stem on the chant pages, all 62 with
    such strokes are breves, and none of the 49 whole notes of the melody has them. Measured from the edge of
    the inside, the head's own rim passed as a stroke on 15 to 33 true whole notes: a column of paper must
    stand between the rim and the stroke. A breve under no letter is taken only on a page that names the ison
    by letters. On a page that marks the ison by breves alone they are the ison's only notes, and the count
    sets such a page as a melody and an ison on one staff: the first form took them and lost them. Alone: 1
    note and 1 syllable gained on the clean copies, none lost, and 8 notes that are not there fewer on the two
    scans of one clean hymn; 5 pages differ; no page of the fresh set.
  - **A whole note at the left of the other voice's note** (`music.BESIDE_OVER`). One print sets a whole note
    of the upper voice where a head with a stem up would stand, at the left of the stem line that the lower
    voice's note hangs on, a third over it. The reader took the two for two moments, and the count showed 5
    notes that are not there on that score. The first form asked only for a head without a stem that stands
    over a stemmed head along the staff. It acted at 15 places of LitChoir's pages. The one on the clean copy
    was the print's. The 14 others, on copies by hand, were marks that are no whole notes (a worn head, a box
    of two beamed notes, a pencil ring, the letters of a heading), and alone the rule lost 7 notes and gained
    2 there, by parts and staff by staff alike, inside a round whose count showed 13 gained and 5 lost. The
    rule asks for the print's whole shape now and changes that one page: four notes of the lower voice begin
    with the upper voice's whole note, 5 notes that are not there fewer, no note gained or lost. A rule for
    one case. Because it is a rule of `music.py`, the 139 pages of the software-made PDFs were read with the
    round's code and set beside main's reading: no page differs.
  - **A trial in the count.** A lone note that the count gives to both parts of a staff is a note that is not
    there in one of them where the other part rests. Not giving it to both where the other part has a gap was
    tried on a copy of the scorer and scored on the stored runs: 1 note gained on the clean copies and 42 lost.
    It was dropped, and the scorer is as it was.
  - **What the rules cost, and what stays.** One note of a copy by hand is lost (ascension-kontakion, second
    scan): a half note is read as a quarter note. A true change to 3/4 in the middle of a staff is read now.
    The page changes the time back with a thin C by hand at the start of a later staff, which the reader does
    not read, and a tie of the votes in a bar after it is decided by 3/4. The rule stays as it is. On three
    other scans a recited note more is set apart beside the gain (4 notes gained by parts there, 2 staff by
    staff): that part of the gain is the count's. What stays on the clean copies, among others: the grace
    notes of one hymn, which are read as notes (about 12 on its two scans), a cell between a breve's bar and
    the stem of a half note, a breve whose bar touches a half note, and on the print of the whole-note rule a
    tenor's note that the count gives to the bass too and a slur that is read as a filled head.
  - The full reading of every page, with the words (`w352v1` against `w349v1`, scored again with the relation):
    clean copies 22,057 of 22,649 notes (97.4 %; 40 at 99 %; 21,925 of 22,606 as typed), 1 gained and none
    lost, 6,062 of 6,254 syllables, 1 gained and none lost; 458 notes that are not there (448 with the places
    that a hand changed apart), 39 fewer by the rules beside the 48 of the count; staff by staff 1 gained and
    none lost. All scores 78,647 of 89,866 (87.5 %; 94 at 99 %): 14 gained and 1 lost by parts, 16 gained and
    1 lost staff by staff, and 96 notes that are not there fewer (4,722 on all scores). 34 pages differ: 84
    notes are no longer written, 5 change their pitch or their length, 4 their moment, none is new. Syllables
    25,544 of 28,695. 30 clean scores read every printed note, 36 every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not
    there, and 20 are whole in all three, as after round 74; 11 lack one to three notes; 588 printed notes are
    lacking (589 before the round with the refrain counted; the 582 of round 74 did not hold the refrain's 9
    and held 2 notes that a hand changed). The fresh set: 39,939 of 41,761 notes (95.6 %; 99 pieces at 99 %),
    8,300 of 11,341 syllables, both as before, and 3 notes that are not there fewer on one piece; its reading
    with the words and its reading of every page agree piece by piece. The kit: 6,229 by parts and 5,799 staff
    by staff, as before.

- Round 74 (20 November 2026): marks on a bar line, the letters f and p, and the sign of cut time.

  The round began on a clean score whose two pages held 42 notes that are not there (nativity-kontakion). They
  were of five kinds, and each kind is a shape that other pages hold too: marks that hang on a bar line, the
  letters f and p of the dynamics, the sign of cut time and the paper between a sharp and its note. Five rules
  came of it, each from a census of every page of LitChoir with each mark looked at. The forms of the round were
  read on every page of LitChoir and of the fresh set, twice, and every note that they changed was set on a sheet
  and looked at. That narrowed two of the rules and mended a third.

  - **A mark on a line of the system** (`symbols.LINE_MARK`). A stem that is 10 staff spaces long or more is no
    stem: it is a bar line through the staves of a system, or the line that opens or ends them. A mark that
    hangs on such a line was read as a note with the line as its stem. Every such mark of LitChoir's pages was
    listed (104, each looked at): a slur that crosses the bar line or ends at it, the tail of a hairpin, a letter
    of the dynamics between two staves, a number of the time signature that stands on the line, the pieces of a
    double bar line, and the cell that the line closes with a staff line and a slur, read as a half note. A hand
    also draws true heads against a bar line, and those are as fat as the page's filled heads: the rule acts only
    on a mark whose fattest ink is under 0.85 of them. The first form took a cell by its level side alone and
    lost 2 notes on a copy by hand, a half note whose rim is the last bar line of its staff. The direction of
    the inside did not part that head from the cells; its size does (0.55 staff spaces squared against 0.25 at
    most). A natural that is printed against a bar line and a loop of a clef at the opening line have closed
    paper of that size too, and stay notes that are not there. The line of a mark that goes is a bar line of its
    staff again, and four lengths on three pages come right with it. Alone: 2 notes gained on the clean copies and
    none lost, 15 notes that are not there fewer there; 27 pages differ; no page of the fresh set differs.
  - **The letter f** (`symbols.F_SIGN`). The stroke of an f of the dynamics (f, ff, mf) was read as a stem and
    its hook as a head beside the staff. Of 3,448 heads that are read from a stroke on LitChoir's pages, 12
    strokes beside the staff have the shape of the letter: short (2.7 staff spaces at most), leaning (0.21 staff
    spaces or more, where the other short strokes with ink across them lean 0.11 at most), with a crossbar. All
    12 are letters. A second step takes the paper that the two letters of an ff close in between them, which was
    read as a hollow head with one letter's stroke as its stem and the crossbar as its ledger line. Its first
    form asked for one stroke of the shape of an f at the mark, was tried on a few pages and went into the full
    reading: it took 38 marks on 30 pages, nearly all of them true notes on a ledger line of copies by hand,
    whose short stems lean, with the ledger line across them (22 notes lost by the count). The step now asks for
    the shape of the thing itself, two such strokes at the two sides of the mark on one crossbar, and takes the
    one mark that it was written for. Alone: 1 note gained on the clean copies and 4 on all scores, none lost, 16
    notes that are not there fewer on the clean copies; 5 pages differ. Every change on the fresh set is this
    rule's (below).
  - **The sign of cut time** (`meter.COMMON_CUT`). The stroke through the C closes it in two or three pieces of
    paper, which were read as whole notes one over the other. Of the 416 pieces of LitChoir's pages with the size
    and the place of a C that close in more than one piece of paper, two are printed signs of cut time (a third,
    on a thick copy, is one blot and is left): the stroke stands out of the C at its top and at its bottom, where
    a sharp and a natural have two strokes and the stem of a head stands out at one end. The first form let
    pieces as high as the sign into the finder of the C of common time, and on one page a flat of the key was
    then grouped with the C beside it, which was no longer tested alone: a new note that is not there, on a page
    that the rule was not written for. A piece as high as the sign of cut time stands alone now. Alone: 1 note
    gained on the clean copies, none lost, 4 notes that are not there fewer on two scores.
  - **The bowl of the letter p** (`symbols.P_BOWL`). The bowl of a p (p, pp, mp) was read as a whole note beside
    the staff. It is the top of its letter, and the bold stroke of the letter goes on under its left half, with
    paper under the rest. Every hollow mark without a stem was measured on every page (1,199 marks); the rule
    takes 9, each looked at: eight letters p and the g of a heading. The upper of two whole notes that touch
    stands on the rim of the lower one as on a stroke, so a mark with a head under it stays. Alone: 1 note gained
    on the clean copies, none lost, 13 notes that are not there fewer on five scores (a mark that both voices of
    a staff were given counts twice); 7 pages differ.
  - **The paper between a sharp and its note** (`signs.SHARP_ROOM`). Where a heavy sharp is joined to its note by
    the ledger line, the paper between the two is closed in and was read as a hollow head that took the sharp.
    A census at the reader's own step listed the 246 hollow marks that lie on a sharp of two strokes: nearly all
    are hollow heads whose own two sides pass for the strokes, and their closed paper ends 3 pixels or more
    before the mark's right edge, at the rim. The paper of one mark begins at the sharp and goes on to the edge.
    A rule for one case: one note that is not there fewer, and no count moves.
  - **The lists by the page.** A hand crossed out printed rests on this score, and the hymn is typed as the
    choir then sang. On one page two chords of dotted half notes stand before a quarter rest that is crossed
    out, and the hymn is typed with whole notes: the page prints dotted half notes, and the list of the notes by
    the page holds them (4 notes, 128 on the list now). On the other page a hand crossed out the dots themselves
    with an X: those two notes go on the list of the notes that a hand changed (4 notes on 2 scores now). Each
    place was looked at in the scan itself. The reading before the round, scored again with the lists, has
    22,008 notes on the clean copies (22,005 before); as typed it gives the figures of round 73 exactly (21,921
    of 22,606).
  - **What the rules cost, and what stays.** Three true notes of copies by hand are read worse, inside scores
    whose count does not fall. Two hollow heads by hand with an open rim, drawn against a bar line, had been read
    as filled quarter notes with the line as their stem (a wrong length each); they go with the marks on a bar
    line and are not read now. On a third page a dotted half note of the soprano is read as a half note: the note
    that is not there on the bar line had stood in the tenor's voice, and without it the tenor's half note,
    whose dot by hand is not read, decides the step. That score gains 8 notes by parts, by the line-up of the
    count, and loses this 1 staff by staff. On the score of the round, an X by pen and a word by hand over the
    staff are still read as notes, as is the triangle between a hairpin and a bar line; a printed head with an
    open rim is not read.
  - **The fresh set.** 51 pages differ, every one by the letter f: 264 notes that are not there fewer (the f, ff
    and mf under the staves of an engraved liturgy, 223 to 183 on one movement and 48 to 5 on another), 18 notes
    gained and 3 lost. The 3 are eighth notes at the end of a bar on one piece, read as quarter notes now: the
    notes that are not there had stood in that bar, and without them the rule that fills a bar
    (`music.BAR_FULL`) makes the last notes longer, because the eighth rest before them is not counted. With
    that rule off they read right. The rule for the letter stays as it is, and the rest is on the list of what is
    left. The four other rules change no page of the fresh set. The reading of the fresh set with the words is a
    chain of readings of the pages that each round changed; set beside the reading of every page, five pieces
    held pages that were older than main (their counts differed by up to 3 notes, and no syllable). They were
    read again, and the two readings agree piece by piece now.
  - The full reading of every page, with the words (`w349v1` against `w346v1`, scored again with the lists):
    clean copies 22,011 of 22,595 notes (97.4 %; 40 at 99 %; 21,924 of 22,606 as typed), 3 gained and none
    lost, 6,051 of 6,244 syllables as before; 545 notes that are not there (535 with the places that a hand
    changed apart), 39 fewer; staff by staff 10 gained and none lost. All scores 78,589 of 89,815 (87.5 %; 94
    at 99 %): 146 gained and none lost by parts. 126 of them are one thick copy whose layout turns
    (st-basil-in-thee-rejoiceth: with a note on a bar line gone, the count gives each of its four staves one
    part, 92 notes are no longer set apart as recited, and 33 more count as not there); 3 are two copies by hand
    whose recited notes set apart grow by 6; 8 are the line-up named above. Without the thick copy: 20 gained and
    none lost by parts, 17 gained and 1 lost staff by staff, and 60 notes that are not there fewer (4,866 on all
    scores with that copy's 33). 34 pages differ: 70 notes are no longer written, 10 change their voice or their
    length, none is new. No syllable differs (25,533 of 28,685). 30 clean scores read every printed note, 36
    every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not there, and 20 are whole in all three, as after round 73;
    11 lack one to three notes; 582 printed notes are lacking. The fresh set: 39,939 of 41,761 notes (95.6 %; 99
    pieces at 99 %), 8,300 of 11,341 syllables as before. The kit: 6,229 by parts and 5,799 staff by staff, as
    before.

- Round 73 (20 November 2026): a slur read as a note, and no ledger line past its head.

  The round began on the typewriter's hymn, where the sheet of mistakes showed a run of notes a step off on one
  of its three scans. All three scans read those places alike: the run was the sheet's own line-up, pushed off by
  one note that is not there, the slur over two beamed notes, read as an eighth note. The sheet showed 19
  mistakes on that score and shows 10 without the note; the count had never held them against the reader (322 of
  326 before and after). Three changes came of the round, each from a census and each counted alone.

  - **A mark between two stem ends** (`symbols.STEM_END_SPAN`). A slur over two beamed notes lies between the ends
    of their two stems, and a bold one was read as a note (on the typewriter's page by the finder that reads a
    head from its stem). Every read note whose mark is long and flat (at least 1.3 heads wide and at most 0.9
    heads high) was listed from the stored notes of all 657 pages, with the scorer's verdict and with the nearest
    stem end of another note at each of its sides: 581 marks. The 8 whose two stems both stand within 1.2 staff spaces are slurs at two beamed notes and
    beams, each looked at; the nearest true note has its stems 1.43 away. The rule of round 56 for a flat mark at
    a stem's end asks for the stem inside the mark's box, and here the stems end beside it. Alone: 1 note gained
    on the clean copies, on all scores and on the fresh set, none lost; 10 notes that are not there fewer on
    LitChoir (9 on the clean copies: a mark that both voices of a staff were given counts twice) and 5 on the
    fresh set, whose 4 marks are slurs at two beamed notes too.
  - **A ledger dash close to the staff** (`symbols.LEDGER_FOOT`, from 0.4 to 0.3 staff spaces). Round 72 left a
    census of every head beside the staff with no ledger line found: 11,616 on all pages, 460 of them with thin
    level ink at a side of their foot. The heads of the first space, which hang on the staff's own line, have that
    ink at most 0.28 staff spaces from the staff, and four true heads on a dash of their own stand at 0.33 to
    0.36. Alone: 1 note on the clean copies and 4 on all scores, none lost, and no page of the fresh set changes.
  - **No ledger line lies past its head** (`symbols.LEDGER_OWN`). The count of a head's ledger lines took every
    level row of ink in the head's columns. Now, where a row stands in the head's box, the rows further from the
    staff than the last such row are not counted. The rule was written for a chord on two ledger lines under a
    pencil ring, which it does not mend (its rows stand at no ledger place). So every head whose rows it cuts was
    listed, 35 on LitChoir's pages, and each was looked at. They are: the head nearer the staff of a chord on two
    ledger lines, which has the other head's line among its rows and was read a step off, between the two; a
    hollow head on its ledger line whose rim past the line one print draws as level and as long as the line (10
    heads that were read a step off: joined with the line, the rim moved the line off the head's middle); a head with
    a slur over it or a row of words; and marks that are no notes (a dynamic mark, the letters of a heading).
    Alone: no note on the clean copies, 11 gained and 3 lost on all scores, 39 gained and none lost on the fresh
    set, on two movements of one engraved liturgy whose tenor and bass stand a third apart on two ledger lines
    (41 heads on 3 pages; four were looked at).
  - The 3 lost notes are two heads on two copies by hand, and the rule stays as it is. A half note that sits on
    top of its dash had its rim counted as a second line, which gave the right place by chance (2 notes of
    forerunner-nativity-kontakion). A filled head that hangs under the staff, with no line at all, had two rows of
    its own ink joined (1 note of palm-hosanna--s3). How far the cut row stands from the row that is kept does not
    part them from the gains: 0.52 and 0.42 staff spaces, against 0.27 to 0.45 at the hollow heads on a line and
    about 0.9 at the chords.
  - The full reading of every page, with the words (`w346v1` against `w345v1`; the scorer and the lists are as in
    round 72): clean copies 22,005 of 22,595 notes (97.4 %; 40 at 99 %; 21,921 of 22,606 as typed), 2 gained and
    none lost, 6,051 of 6,244 syllables as before; all scores 78,440 of 89,730 (94 at 99 %), 16 gained and 3 lost,
    staff by staff 15 gained and 4 lost; 584 notes that are not there on the clean copies (574 with the places
    that a hand changed apart) and 4,893 on all scores, 9 and 12 fewer. 43 pages differ. One syllable more counts
    on a copy by hand, with a note whose pitch changes (25,533 of 28,685). 30 clean scores read every printed
    note, 36 every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not there, and 20 are whole in all three, as after
    round 72; 11 lack one to three notes. The fresh set: 39,924 of 41,761 notes (95.6 %; 98 pieces at 99 %), 40
    gained and none lost on 7 pages, 6 notes that are not there fewer, and no syllable differs (8,300 of 11,341).
    The kit: 6,229 by parts as before (2 gained and 2 lost) and 5,799 staff by staff (5,801 before; 1 gained and
    3 lost, the half note on its dash).

- Round 72 (19 November 2026): the notes of a music typewriter's print.

  Four of the clean scores are the same hymn set on a music typewriter, and two more scores are of its sister
  print. Their pages lost notes in the same four ways, and each way is a shape, not a limit. Four rules came of
  it. The first forms were read on every page of LitChoir and of the fresh set, every place that they changed was
  cut out of its page and looked at, two of them were narrowed on LitChoir's own measures because the fresh set
  lost notes, and the fourth rule was then written, measured by a census of every piece that it cuts.

  - **Two heads at one stem line** (`symbols.SIDE_TWO`). Where two voices sing one note with different lengths,
    this print sets their heads side by side, the upper voice at the left with its stem up and the lower at the
    right with its stem down. The two heads are one mark, and upright ink leaves it at all four corners: the
    reader called it a sharp. Now such a mark is cut at the column of its stems where it is from 1.5 to 2.4 heads
    wide and at most 1.3 heads high, the four ways stand within a quarter of a staff space of one column, that
    column lies inside the mark, and each part is a head: a third of the box of a head in ink, and a circle of ink
    at least 0.55 of a head's height in it. The last measure came from the fresh set, where the first form read
    the strokes of a tremolo across a stem as two heads. Alone: 9 notes on the clean copies, 19 on all scores
    (2 lost), nothing on the fresh set.
  - **A whole note that sits on its ledger line** (`symbols.WHOLE_FOOT`). This print draws the first ledger line
    no wider than the note, so a whole note beside the staff shows no line at either side, and the reader asks for
    a long stem where it finds no ledger line. Now a hollow oval with no stem whose edge toward the staff is a
    level line as wide as the mark, 0.4 to 1.2 staff spaces from the staff, is such a note. The bowl of the letter
    p of a dynamic mark is that shape too, and the fresh set showed it: the mark and its line must stand alone in
    paper (the piece of ink that holds them goes on at most 0.15 staff spaces over and under and 0.7 at the sides,
    which the eleven true notes of LitChoir all do). Alone: 3 notes on the clean copies, 9 on all scores, nothing
    on the fresh set.
  - **Ledger lines that stand closer than the staff lines** (`symbols.LEDGER_CLOSE`). This print sets its ledger
    lines about 0.7 of a staff space apart, so a head on the third line measures a third too low and the reader
    did not trust the count of the lines. The page shows its own gap: the median gap over its heads whose count
    held. Where that is at most 0.85 of a staff space, a count of two lines or more holds when each of its own
    gaps is within 0.15 of the page's gap, the place measured with that gap is within one step of the count, and
    the lines are dashes of one length, one over the other (a slur runs on). Alone: 4 notes on the clean copies, 5
    on all scores, nothing on the fresh set.
  - **A head on its dash glued to a head on the staff** (`symbols.DASH_PAIR`). With the ledger line that close,
    the head on it and the head on the staff's first line run into each other, and the piece is too low to be cut
    as two heads of a chord. Such a piece is 1.35 to 1.95 heads high, ends half a head past the staff's line, its
    other head's middle lies 0.3 to 0.85 staff spaces beyond that line, and the ledger line shows there as thin
    level ink that leaves the head at a side. Alone: 10 notes on the clean copies, 16 on all scores, nothing on
    the fresh set.

  A census of all 37 pieces that the fourth rule cuts, each looked at, showed what the counts hid. Two half notes
  a third apart with the staff line between them are the same shape, and one such chord was cut: the insides tell,
  and a piece that shuts in paper of a head's inside size (0.15 staff spaces squared; the chord held 0.28 and
  every true pair 0.09 at most) is no pair (`symbols.DASH_SHUT`). The two heads of a pair are also the one mark
  that they were, for the page's measure of a hollow head's inside, for the rule for a whole note beside a stemmed
  head and for the rule for a whole note that touches a chord (`symbols.DASH_OWN`): counted twice, a pair of one
  copy by hand moved the page's measure and a sign at a clef was read as a half note, the lower head of a pair
  gave its stem away because its ledger line makes it wider, and a head whose inside the ledger line cuts into
  slivers lost its stem. Each guard alone: the first brings a right pitch back and takes a false note away on one
  score, the second brings a right note, mends a length and takes two false notes away on another. Two steps that
  were written and changed no note on any page are off and say so in the code: a stem for the head of a pair that
  shows none (the stem runs along both heads and each was followed into it already), and a guard that asks for the
  whole piece of ink at a mark with two heads.
  - Also counted alone: the guard that asks the paper between the stem line of a pair, the staff line and the head
    beside it to be no head of its own (2 notes on two copies by hand, and two false notes fewer on each), and the
    guard that looks for the ledger lines of a pair past the whole pair (no note; it mends the pitch of one head
    on a copy by hand).
  - By the page. The three scans of the typewriter's hymn print a filled head on the top line at the right of the
    stem line where the bass is typed a fifth lower, and the ring that mends a punched hole hides a whole note of
    the bass on all three: the first goes on the list of the notes by the page (124 notes in 34 scores now), the
    second on the list of what a scan does not show. The ring costs the three scores 2 notes that are not there,
    because the note that had been lined up with the hidden one now counts as a note that is not there.
  - The full reading of every page, with the words (`w345v1`, against `w340v1` scored again with the lists of this
    round, which gives the figures of round 71 as typed exactly): clean copies 22,003 of 22,595 notes (97.4 %; 40
    at 99 %; 21,919 of 22,606 as typed), 6,051 of 6,244 syllables as before (no syllable changes on any page); all
    scores 78,427 of 89,730 (94 at 99 %), staff by staff 51 gained and 2 lost; 593 notes that are not there on the
    clean copies (583 with the places that a hand changed apart) and 4,905 on all scores. 26 notes gained on the
    clean copies and none lost; 49 and 2 on all scores. The two lost stand on one thick copy by hand whose page
    holds other music than its typed hymn: its blot of two voices is read as two heads now, which is what the page
    shows, and the line-up of a score that reads 11 of its 62 notes moved with it. 30 clean scores read every
    printed note, 36 every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not there, and 20 are whole in all three, as
    after round 71; 11 lack one to three notes. The fresh set: no note and no syllable differ (39,884 of 41,761
    notes, 8,300 of 11,341 syllables); one page differs in its layers only. The kit: 6,229 by parts and 5,801
    staff by staff (6,227 and 5,799 before), both by the two heads at one stem line on the typewriter's page.

- Round 71 (19 November 2026): whole notes that touch, and what else the nearly whole scores lack.

  The round went on with the clean scores that lack few notes, place by place. Six rules came of it, and 8 notes
  went on the list of the notes by the page. The first forms of the six rules gained 20 notes on all scores and
  lost 5. Every note that differed between the two readings was then cut out of its page and looked at: 79 places
  on 37 pages. 25 were right for the reason that their rule gives. The others were wrong, were notes that are not
  there before and after, or were notes that a changed measure of the page moved or brought by chance. Four rules
  were narrowed by what those places showed, and one was mended. The final forms change 31 places on 15 pages (the
  25, and 6 notes that are not there before and after), gain 18 notes on all scores and lose none.

  | Rule | Clean copies | All scores | Fresh set |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | The inside of a whole note whose rim another whole note presses thin (`layers.WHOLE_OVER`) | 2 notes gained, none lost | 2 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | Whole notes one over the other: the side of the upper one is no stem of the lower (`symbols.WHOLE_STACK`) | 4 gained, none lost; 1 fewer that is not there | 6 gained, none lost; 1 fewer that is not there | no page differs |
  | A stem that a white strip of the copy cuts off before its beam (`symbols.STEM_BROKEN`) | 2 gained, none lost | 2 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | A whole note without a dot that touches the dotted half notes of another voice (`symbols.WHOLE_DOTLESS`) | 1 gained, none lost | 1 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | The strokes of a sharp without the staff lines that they were followed into (`signs.PAIR_LINE`) | 1 gained, none lost | 3 gained, none lost; 4 fewer that are not there | no page differs |
  | A half note on a line whose inside leans: the line cuts it into a half at each side (`symbols.HALVES_ACROSS`) | 2 gained, none lost | 4 gained, none lost | no page differs |

  - Whole notes that touch. extra-beatitudes ends its lines on chords of whole notes, and six of those notes were
    not read: three causes, each with its rule. Two whole notes a third apart touch, and each rim is thinner where
    the other note presses on it: the inside of the upper one was a cell between two lines and two strokes for the
    layers, with a side too thin for a whole note (`layers.WHOLE_INSIDE`). Such a cell is the inside of a whole note
    where its sides are 0.25 staff spaces thick or more and the inside of a whole note stands in the next cell,
    across one staff line. The first form took the cell beside any inside that had been taken: 10 cells on
    LitChoir's pages, and 9 of them were loops of a clef by hand. Each moved notes far from the clef, because the
    page's own measure of a hole comes from its read insides (one copy by hand lost a note at the other end of a
    staff). Beside the inside of a whole note the rule acts on the one page.
  - Whole notes in line. Where whole notes stand one over the other, the sides of the upper ones were followed from
    the lowest one as its stem, over the staff lines between them, and the lowest was a half note. A stem of a
    hollow oval is none where it ends in the rows of another hollow oval without a stem, whose edges stand in line
    with its own. The first form took 32 stems back on 16 pages. 4 were such stacks. 2 were the stems of true half
    notes: the paper that a slur, a stem and the staff lines close in is a hollow mark without a stem too, and so is
    a whole note of the next staff under a short stem by hand. 11 were strokes of sharps, whose cells are hollow
    marks in line, and two more whole notes that are not there stood in each of two sharps of a heavy print. 7 were
    the stems of the 4s of a time signature by hand. The census gave two measures. The other oval is as wide against
    its height as a whole note. And the stem has no ink of its own: outside the ovals and outside the staff lines
    its column holds ink in at most 0.25 staff spaces (0.17 at most in the four stacks, 0.35 or more at a sharp,
    whose strokes go on past its cells). What still changes beside the four stacks: the 4s of two time signatures by
    hand, read as notes before and after.
  - A whole note beside dotted half notes. One chord of extra-beatitudes sets a whole note of the bass under two
    dotted half notes of the tenor, and all three were read on one stem. The rule of round 61 for such a whole note
    (`symbols.CHORD_WHOLE`) asks how its inside leans, and this inside leans as a half note's does. The page shows
    it otherwise: the heads of a chord have their dots together, and a print sets a whole note wider than a half
    note. Of 162 pairs of hollow heads on one stem on LitChoir's scan pages, 5 have a dot at the other heads only;
    the whole note is wider than the others by 0.26 staff spaces, and the four others by 0.12 at most. The first
    form also acted at one head that was read twice, which was its own neighbour, and at a head on a ledger line,
    where the line and a dot at its end are one wide mark with the head: the heads now stand a third apart or more,
    and the head stands in the staff. It is a rule for one case.
  - The stem at a white strip. non-liturgical-o-pure-virgin--s2 has a strip across one page that the copy left
    white, and the strip cuts two stems off before their beam: two eighth notes were quarters. The first form led
    any stem on that breaks off before a beam, over a gap of paper in its own column, and gained 26 notes on all
    scores and lost 272: past the end of a stem stand a second beam, the line of a bracket and a bold slur over two
    stems, which is as thick as a beam. A second form (no flag or beam where the stem breaks off, a short gap)
    gained 31 and lost 94. A census of the 88 stems that it led on over a gap in their own column alone: 3 right and
    81 wrong. The rule now asks for the cause itself: the rows of the gap hold no ink over the whole staff. It acts
    on the two stems. Two worn stems of another clean score, which the short gap had read, are given up.
  - The sharp. A sharp that touches the staff lines is followed into them, and its strokes then end in the lines, at
    other heights than the strokes themselves do: the two made no sign. Two strokes that make no sign are measured
    again without an end piece that lies in the rows of a staff line with paper between it and the rest, and are a
    sharp where they then stand as the strokes of a sharp do. liturgy-we-praise-thee reads the sharp that it lacked,
    and in the two scans of one hymn a heavy sharp that was read as a half note is the sharp of its note (2 notes
    that are not there fewer in each scan). On a thick copy the first form read the two sides of the rim of a hollow
    head as the strokes of a sharp, inside the head: a sharp of cut strokes stands before its head, as round 59
    found for a joined sign.
  - The leaning half note. carol-great-rejoicing lacked one half note, on a staff line: the inside of a printed half
    note rises to the right, so the line cuts it into a half over the line at the right and a half under it at the
    left. The two halves share few columns, and were neither one inside nor two pieces one over the other. Two
    pieces of closed paper at the two sides of one line are the inside of a head on that line where the upper one
    lies right of the lower one, they share a column, both together are at most 1.2 staff spaces wide and 1.0 high,
    and the line between them is ink. Such a head takes its own stem. Where the head over or under it had been read
    with both stems, one up and one down, the two share them: the upper head keeps the stem that goes up, the lower
    one the stem that goes down. The first form gave a new lower head the upper head's stem on one copy by hand, and
    that score lost a note although its page was read more truly. The rule makes four heads on LitChoir's pages, all
    true: the one of the carol, a half note under the staff in non-liturgical-jesus-prayer, and two on copies by
    hand.
  - By the page. wedding-isaiah prints two plain quarter notes in its lower staff, for both of its voices, under
    the dotted quarter and the eighth of the upper staff (4 notes; the bar adds up in both staves).
    carol-great-rejoicing prints one quarter note in the bass under two beamed eighths of the tenor, where the hymn
    is typed with two eighths. extra-beatitudes prints a half note D with a slur to a half note C under the fermata
    for the bass, where the hymn is typed with one whole note. The list holds 121 notes in 34 scores now, 85 of
    them on the clean copies, and brings 7 notes on the clean copies by itself (21,965 of 22,598 before the rules).
  - The full reading of the pages that change, with the words (`w340v1`, against `w335v1` scored again with the list
    of this round): clean copies 21,977 of 22,598 notes (97.3 %; 39 at 99 %; 21,896 of 22,606 as typed), 6,051 of
    6,244 syllables as before (no syllable changes on any page); all scores 78,380 of 89,732 (93 at 99 %), staff by
    staff 16 gained and none lost; 596 notes that are not there on the clean copies (586 with the places that a hand
    changed apart) and 4,912 on all scores (598 and 4,918 before; 1 of each by the list). carol-great-rejoicing
    reads every printed note now, extra-beatitudes gains 6 notes, non-liturgical-o-pure-virgin--s2 2, and
    communion-lords-prayer, liturgy-we-praise-thee and non-liturgical-jesus-prayer 1 each; no score loses a note. 30
    clean scores read every printed note, 36 every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not there, and 20 are
    whole in all three. On the copy by hand whose new half note belongs to the ison, the line-up of the ison's equal
    notes moved, and the scorer sets one more of them apart as recited: the drawn notes of all scores fall by 1
    (89,732). The fresh set: no page differs, with the first forms and with the final ones (39,884 of 41,761 notes,
    8,300 of 11,341 syllables). The kit: 6227 by parts and 5799 staff by staff, as before.

  Counted and left:
  - The sign of cut time and a 4/4 whose two 4s share one narrow stem are read as notes (2 and 3 that are not
    there). The 4s of two time signatures by hand are read as whole notes now, as half notes before.
  - The edge of a pasted slip gives 2 false heads on one page. Neither the grey of the middle of a head nor the
    height of the mark parts them from the 13,687 filled heads that were counted.
  - The arc of a fermata is read as a note in two places. Its dot is found at one of them, and the note moves no
    count by parts.
  - Three naturals of a dense print stand glued between two heads that touch. One print draws its dots as commas 0.9
    staff spaces over the middle of the head (5 notes). A dot at the lowest head of a chord only, by the page: 5
    such chords of 162, and 2 of those dots are false.
  - Five half notes that a heavy print closed; two worn stems that end before their beam without a strip; a vote of
    two staves that is tied; a binder hole over a beam; the false notes under a paperclip and a pencil ring.
  - A heavy sharp before a quarter note still gives one whole note that is not there in each of two scans (the
    lowest cell of the sharp has no stroke of its own for the rules of the signs to find), and the bowl of a flat on
    a thick copy is read as a half note.

- Round 70 (19 November 2026): the scores that are nearly whole.

  After round 69, 13 clean scores lacked one to three notes, and others lacked one syllable or held one note that
  is not there. The round took these scores one by one: what each still lacks was listed with its place on the
  page, and a place got a rule where the page shows a shape that a rule can ask for.

  | Rule | Clean copies | All scores | Fresh set |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | A head that two voices share is tied twice over the end of a line (`ties.TIE_BREAK_BOTH`) | 1 note gained, none lost; 1 fewer that is not there | 1 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | The first word of a row is the rest of the word that the row before leaves open (`ocr.MEND_BEFORE`) | 2 syllables gained, none lost | 5 gained, none lost | 1 gained, none lost |
  | Two stems that meet end to end: the head with the short stem is a mark at the end of the other's stem (`symbols.STEM_MEET`) | no note moves; 1 fewer that is not there | no note moves; 7 fewer that are not there | no page differs |
  | A ragged slit far beside the inside of a wide hollow head is the inside of a second half note (`symbols.SLIT_BESIDE`) | 1 gained, none lost | 1 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | A staff that shows no clef begins where the staff of its own system begins that shows one (`starts.SYSTEM_START`) | 2 gained, none lost; 1 fewer that is not there | 2 gained, none lost | no page differs |

  - The tie. megalynarion-bulgarian ends a line on a head that two voices share, with a stem up and a stem down,
    and a bow leaves it over the end of the line. The next line sets the two voices side by side, because their
    notes differ in length there: a whole note of one voice beside the shorter note of the other, each with a
    coming bow. The tie over the end of a line joined the first of the two only, and the whole note was struck
    again. The rule ties the head of the other voice too where it stands at the same place beside the first (it
    begins at most 0.3 staff spaces inside the first one's box and 0.5 after it) and a bow comes to it as well. It
    is a rule for one case: one page of LitChoir changes, and none of the fresh set.
  - The word. megalynarion-bulgarian lacked two syllables, both the first word of a row: the row before ends
    inside a word ("Moth-", "glo-"), and the row begins with the rest of it ("er", "ri-ous"). The rest alone is no
    word of the list, so it was mended into one ("pi-ous"), or another reading won that held only words of the
    list ("were"). Every stored reading of both rows held the true word. The step now hands each row the letters
    of the word that the first row under the same part's staff in the system before leaves open. A first word
    that the list holds together with those letters is not mended alone. One misread letter of it is mended by
    the whole word, where Tesseract is not sure of the word and both pieces have two letters or more. And where
    Tesseract is not sure of the first word and it is no such rest, the first word of another reading that is one
    takes its place, with the words of the row that lie in its columns (they are pieces of it). The first form
    also mended a piece of one letter and gained 5 syllables on all scan pages and lost 2: a stray stroke that is
    read as one letter became the end of the word before it ("has-" and "v" as "hast"). With two letters or more
    it gains the same 5 and loses none; on the clean copies 2 and none, on the fresh set 1 and none. The rule
    changes 11 rows of LitChoir and 1 of the fresh set, and each was looked at, because a syllable too many costs
    nothing in the count: 9 are right now, and in 3 a stray mark before the true word is read as another stray
    word than before.
  - The stems. wedding-georgian read every note and syllable and one note that is not there: a tick by pen at
    the end of a stem, read as a small head with a short stem of its own. The stem of a chord leaves all of its
    heads at one end, so two stems that meet end to end in one column, one going down from the upper head and
    one going up from the lower, are one stroke with a head at each end, and no chord. A count from the stored
    notes found four such pairs on all pages of LitChoir and none on the fresh set, each looked at: the tick, the
    end of a tie's bow, and two strokes by hand. The head whose stem is shorter than 2 staff spaces goes where
    the other's is 2.5 or longer. Three pages change (the fourth pair is past the limits): the three false notes
    go, and four more notes that are not there go with them on two other scans, where the false note had moved
    the moments of its bar.
  - The second half note. pascha-receive-short lacked a half note that stands a second under another half note
    of the same voice, at the other side of the stem. The two heads are one mark, and only one of their two
    insides is round enough to be filled as the inside of a head; the other is a ragged slit, and a slit in the
    box of a hollow head that was read is taken for that head's own (`symbols.RAGGED_SLIT`). A count of every
    such slit of the clean pages found 15 in a wide hollow head, and 2 are a second head: there the box is at
    least 1.5 head widths wide and the slit lies at least 0.6 head widths beside the middle of the head's own
    inside. The new head takes a stem of its own or hangs on the other head's stem, and next to the staff it
    needs no ledger line of its own. Read on every page it acts at four places: the two of the census (the other
    is in nativity-troparion--s2, whose two voices at that moment are read as the page prints them now: 2 notes
    more staff by staff, none by parts), a ring by pen beside a printed quarter note on another scan (the pitch
    is right now, the length is not), and a chord that a hand drew over, where the new head took a syllable from
    its note in the full reading with the words. That chord holds four pieces of filled inside in the box of the
    read head; the true places hold two at most (the head's own inside, which a staff line cuts). With that limit
    the chord reads as before.
  - The start of a staff. megalynarion-yaichkov lacked two notes, the first notes of two staves on one page. The
    scan cuts the left edge of that page, and the lower systems lose their clefs. A staff that shows no clef and
    no key of its own is given the room that the page's other staves take from their left ends, and here that
    room is measured from the cut: it reached past the first note. The other staff of each system still shows the
    start that the scan left, the flat of its key, and its notes begin two staff spaces earlier. The rule gives a staff without a start of its
    own the start of a staff of its own system that shows one, where that staff begins within one staff space of
    the same left end and its notes begin one staff space or more earlier. A count of the stored pages found 115
    systems on 92 scan pages whose staves begin more than 1.5 staff spaces apart. Read alone, each page with the
    rule in its widest form, 99 staves on 66 pages would begin elsewhere: 72 later and 27 earlier. A later start
    takes notes away and was not taken. The earlier start of one staff space or more changed two pages in the
    reading of the hymns: megalynarion-yaichkov's, and a page by hand whose staves are found at odd places (5
    notes more, none true), which the limit for the left end keeps out. Read on every page of LitChoir and of the
    fresh set, the rule changes the one page. It is a rule for one page of the clean copies; a page whose scan
    cuts the clefs of every staff of a system is not helped by it.
  - The list of the notes by the page. nativity-canon-ode-9 read every note and one that is not there: a quarter
    note of the alto before the last whole note, which the page prints with a stem up and a stem down, as it
    prints the note that both voices share earlier in the same staff. The typed hymn gives the alto no note
    there. The list holds such a note now (`own`: a note that the page prints for a part at a moment where the
    typed hymn gives the part none): 113 notes in 32 scores, 77 of them on the clean copies. The count of the
    drawn notes grows by 1 (22,598 on the clean copies).
  - The full reading of the pages that change, with the words (`w335v1`, against `w330v1`): clean copies 21,958
    of 22,598 notes (97.2 %; 37 at 99 %; 21,884 of 22,606 as typed), 6,051 of 6,244 syllables (96.9 %; 5,997 of
    6,259 as typed; 37 scores at 99 % of their syllables, 36 before); all scores 78,355 of 89,733 (91 at 99 %),
    staff by staff 6 gained and none lost; 25,532 of 28,685 syllables (19,879 of 22,580 on the scans); 598 notes
    that are not there on the clean copies (588 with the places that a hand changed apart) and 4,918 on all
    scores (602 and 4,928 before). megalynarion-bulgarian, megalynarion-yaichkov and wedding-georgian are whole
    now: 29 clean scores read every printed note, 36 every syllable, 23 both, 27 have no note that is not there,
    and 20 are whole in all three. The fresh set: 39,884 of 41,761 notes as before (no page differs), and 8,300
    of 11,341 syllables (1 gained). The kit: 6227 by parts and 5799 staff by staff (1 gained in each count).

  Counted and left:
  - pascha-receive-short still lacks two half notes a third apart on the top line and its ledger line, worn to
    pieces of their rims.
  - Small pale marks that are read as notes. Marks that are paler than their page, under 0.52 of the area of the
    page's filled heads and under 0.6 of their fatness: 8 false notes and no true one on the clean copies, and 62
    true ones against 25 false on the other scans. No rule.
  - forerunner-nativity-apolytikion: two filled heads a third apart on one stem close a speck of paper between
    them, and the upper head is read as a half note a step low. extra-rejoice-o-virgin: a head that two voices
    share as eighth notes, with a flag at each stem; the thick start of the upper flag is read as the head of the
    second voice. One place each.
  - A pencil ring around a chord (nativity-canon-ode-5), a grey wavy line by hand (wedding-alleluia-grechaninov),
    and a faint dotted half note of a pale print that is read as a quarter note a semitone off
    (pascha-christ-is-risen-appalachian).
  - lazarus-koinonikon's three notes that are not there are the count's: it gives the top of a bass chord to the
    tenor, who holds a long note (the rule of the scorer for it lost in round 66).
  - A first word after an open piece of a word that the list lacks (presentation-christ-apolytikion): the hymn
    that is read is left out of the list, and the word is this hymn's.
  - A stray mark at the bracket of a system, read as a word before the rest of an open word: the rest is read,
    and the stray word stays (3 rows). It costs nothing in the count and is wrong in the result.

- Round 69 (19 November 2026): the stroke of a sign close behind the stem of the note before it.

  After round 68, 54 notes of the clean copies were read a semitone off, and about 15 of them have a printed sign
  that is not read. forerunner-troparion lacked two notes, both such signs, and the pairing of strokes finds both
  signs: they are lost in the step that gives a sign to its note (`signs.note_signs`). Every sign that the pairing
  finds on the 175 clean pages was followed through that step (1,077: 409 go to a head, 373 have no head before
  them, 169 lie on a head, 65 are a heavy stroke on a head, 60 lose a stroke as the stem of a head), and the dropped
  ones were set beside the notes that are read a semitone off. Four dropped signs stand before such a note.

  | Rule | Clean copies | All scores | Fresh set |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | A stroke that a column of paper parts from the stem of a head is no part of that stem (`signs.STEM_APART`) | 2 gained, none lost | 3 gained, none lost | 3 gained, none lost |
  | A mark that begins a head's width before a sign and ends where the sign ends is the head before it, with a bar of the sign glued to it (`signs.GLUED_BEFORE`) | 1 gained, none lost | 1 gained, none lost | no page differs |
  | A head that was read from its stem is no head where that stem is a stroke of a sign of two strokes (`signs.THIN_SIGN`, off) | 2 gained, none lost; 4 fewer that are not there | 2 gained, none lost | none gained, 6 lost |

  - The stroke. The step takes a stroke for the stem of a head where the stem's column lies within 0.3 staff
    spaces of the stroke, and such a stroke is no stroke of a sign. The left stroke of a natural or a flat that
    stands close behind the stem of the note before it, under that note's beam or slur, is as near
    (forerunner-troparion's natural; the flat between two beamed eighths of orthros-doxology-t6, its second scan),
    and so is the right stroke of a natural close before the stem of its own note, which then left a flat
    (st-basil-in-thee-rejoiceth, its second scan). The stem's column was that of its far end, and a stem that was
    followed into a slur ends 8 pixels beside its own ink there. The rule measures from the stem's middle column,
    and the stroke is no part of the stem where a column between the two holds ink, without the staff lines, in
    under a quarter of the rows that they share. A stroke that is the stem's own ink has no such column. The rule
    changes 3 pages of LitChoir and 3 of the fresh set, each looked at: the three of the fresh set are naturals
    close behind the stem of a beamed note, and the middle of one had been read as a note.
  - The bar. The upper bar of forerunner-troparion's sharp touches the head before it, and the head and the bar are
    one mark that ends where the sign ends. A sign that lies on a head is none (the strokes are the head's own
    ink) unless the head reaches on past it, as the sign's own head does when the two touch. Of the 169 signs of
    the clean pages that lie on a head, 9 lie only on a mark that begins before them and ends by their end; the
    one that begins a head's width before its sign and ends with it is the sharp, and none of the other 8 stands
    before a note that is read a semitone off. The rule is a rule for one case of the clean pages: it changes one
    page of LitChoir and none of the fresh set.
  - The head (off). A head is read from an upright stroke with a small piece of ink at its end
    (`symbols.thin_heads`), and the stroke of a sign has such a piece where a slur ends under it
    (nativity-god-is-with-us under a sharp, triodion-meatfare under a flat) or at its own bar. The head is a note
    that is not there, and its stem is no stroke of the sign any more. The first form took every such head whose
    stem stands in the columns of a sign of two strokes, and took a true head of wedding-psalm-128 over a natural
    that stands lower on the staff (2 notes lost); with the strokes in the rows of the sign it gained 2 notes on
    LitChoir and lost none, took 4 notes that are not there from the clean copies and changed 3 pages. On the
    fresh set it lost 6 notes on one piece: a true head that was read from its stem, with a stroke beside the
    stem, became a flat that holds for its bar. No page of LitChoir shows such a head to narrow the rule by. A
    census of every note that was read from its stem (3,034 on all scores: 2,044 right, 267 not there) gave no
    measure either: the two false heads are 0.70 and 0.54 of the height of their page's heads, and 275 true ones
    are under 0.7. The rule is off.
  - The full reading of the pages that change, with the words (`w330v1`, against `w324v1`): clean copies 21,953
    of 22,597 notes (97.2 %; 37 at 99 %; 21,880 of 22,606 as typed), 6,049 of 6,244 syllables as before (5,995 of
    6,259 as typed); all scores 78,350 of 89,732 (91 at 99 %), staff by staff 4 gained and none lost; 602 notes
    that are not there on the clean copies (592 with the places that a hand changed apart) and 4,928 on all
    scores, as before. forerunner-troparion is whole now: 27 clean scores read every printed note, 35 every
    syllable, 21 both, 23 have no note that is not there, and 17 are whole in all three. The fresh set: 39,884 of
    41,761 notes (95.5 %; 97 pieces at 99 %), 4,191 that are not there, as before. The kit as before (6226 by
    parts, 5798 staff by staff): no page of its hymns changes.
